For each state and index, suppose every coordinate of the finite vector of transition and stopping probabilities is measurable in the $\lambda$-completion of the Borel sigma field. A terminal output law is a Borel probability measure on $S^{d-1}$ indexed only by its terminal state and stopping index. Decisions made without a new sample also depend only on the state and index.*

*There are everywhere-defined Borel transition and stopping kernels, with the same state sets and terminal output laws, whose $\pi$-prior joint law of the signal, state path, stopping index and output is the same as for the original rules. The domination <a href="#h:stream:pair-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:stream:pair-domination">[h:stream:pair-domination]</a> holds when $\pi=\sigma$ is uniform spherical probability, for every $d\ge2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-stream-borel"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-stream-borel</summary>

*Proof.* We first construct versions for a fixed set of rules. If $f:\mathbb R^d\times\mathbb R\to[0,1]$ is measurable in the $\lambda$-completion, each dyadic superlevel set $\{f\ge k2^{-n}\}$, $1\le k\le2^n$, agrees with a Borel set outside a Borel $\lambda$-null set, by the description of completion in Tao (Tao 2011, Exercise 1.4.26) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[TaoMeasure2011](#ref-TaoMeasure2011)]. Replace these superlevel sets by their Borel representatives in $$f_n=2^{-n}\sum_{k=1}^{2^n}\mathbf 1_{\{f\ge k2^{-n}\}}.$$ The resulting Borel functions have a Borel $\limsup$ equal to $f$ outside the union of the countably many null sets, since $f_n\to f$. Thus each coordinate of each finite kernel has a Borel version. For one state and index, choose all of its coordinate versions simultaneously. The Borel set on which the resulting vector is not nonnegative with sum one is null; on this set replace the vector by a fixed point mass. The result is a Borel probability vector at every sample pair. There are finitely many coordinates, states and indices, so one Borel $\lambda$-null set $Z$ contains every pair where any coordinate was changed. Sample-free decisions and terminal output laws require no such modification.

Pre-generate all $T$ input rows, including rows after an early stop. Every pair $(X_t,\langle X_t,S\rangle)$ has the marginal law in <a href="#h:stream:pair-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:stream:pair-domination">[h:stream:pair-domination]</a>, so $$\mathbb P\{(X_t,\langle X_t,S\rangle)\in Z\}=0.$$ For a state $u$ chosen from earlier observations, the event that the learner is in $u$ before sample $t$ and this pair lies in $Z$ is a subset of the displayed null event. We do not need a density for a sample conditioned on a particular state. A finite union over $t$ shows that no changed pair is visited almost surely. Couple the original and modified finite choices by the same uniform random numbers. Starting from the same initial state, induction makes their states and stopping decisions equal almost surely. At an equal terminal state and index the output laws are identical, so use their diagonal coupling. This proves the asserted joint-law equality. When $T=0$ there is no sample pair to modify and the same conclusion is immediate.

It remains to check the spherical prior. Conditional on a nonzero $X=x$, rotate coordinates so that $x/\|x\|$ is the first coordinate vector. Write a uniform sphere point as $G/\|G\|$ for a standard Gaussian vector $G=(G_1,G_\perp)$. When $d\ge2$, $R=\|G_\perp\|>0$ almost surely and is independent of $G_1$. For each fixed $R>0$, the map $$g\longmapsto \frac{g}{\sqrt{g^2+R^2}}$$ is a smooth strictly increasing bijection from $\mathbb R$ to $(-1,1)$ with nonzero derivative. The image of the Gaussian law of $G_1$ therefore has a Lebesgue density. Mixing over $R$ and then multiplying by $\|x\|$ shows that $\langle x,S\rangle$ is absolutely continuous for every $x\ne0$. The point $x=0$ has $\gamma_1$-measure zero. Fubini’s theorem applied to the sections of a $\lambda$-null Borel set now proves <a href="#h:stream:pair-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:stream:pair-domination">[h:stream:pair-domination]</a>. ◻

</details>

The equality in Lemma <a href="#h:stream:borel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:borel">15</a> concerns the experiment under the chosen prior. It need not preserve behavior at every exceptional individual signal. In particular, an every-signal success premise will be averaged over the prior before versions are chosen.

We now return to the model’s original jointly measurable randomized experiment. Fix any Borel prior $\pi$ satisfying <a href="#h:stream:pair-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:stream:pair-domination">[h:stream:pair-domination]</a>, and draw the signal from $\pi$ independently of the rows and the model’s data-independent randomness. The preceding lemma supplies the domination condition for the uniform-sphere prior. Write $W$ for the shared rule seed, taking $W$ constant when no seed is used, and $I$ for its initial state. The observable signal, rows, state path, stopping index, and output take values in standard Borel spaces. For a Borel terminal score $\ell$, its original conditional expectation is a measurable function of $W$ and integrates to the mixed expected score. Since $(I,W)$ is jointly independent of the signal and all pre-generated rows, conditional on almost every $W=w$ those inputs retain their joint law and $I$ is independent of them. The dependence allowed between $I$ and $W$ does not affect this conclusion.

Fix such a seed value for which the model’s per-seed kernel hypotheses also hold. Lemma <a href="#h:stream:borel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:borel">15</a> replaces its completed transition and stopping coordinates by everywhere-defined Borel kernels while preserving the original conditional $\pi$-prior law. Keep its terminal output laws. At index $T$, force the sample-free action to stop at every state, retaining each prescribed state/index output law. Assign a fixed unit output only where no law was specified, a case absent from original terminating paths. This does not change the original conditional experiment, which already stops by $T$. It makes the horizon convention in the continuation construction valid even at states absent from that experiment.

The preceding continuation lemma now applies to this fixed-seed Borel learner with any Borel terminal score $\ell$. A numerical score bound uniform over its initial states can be averaged against the conditional law of $I$. If the bound is also uniform in the fixed seed, the final seed average uses the original conditional expected scores. Borel versions may be chosen separately for each seed; no jointly measurable choice of these replacements is required.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The quantitative success bound

We apply the block bound to the fresh continuations just constructed. At every step, all terminal destinations have the original terminal local bound, and all continuing destinations have the inductive bound at that boundary. The following statement records the resulting finite-horizon estimate before we extract the sample lower bound.

<a id="stmt-h-stream-success"></a>
<div id="h:stream:success" class="proposition">

<a id="h:stream:success-bound"></a>
**Proposition 16** (Success after a bounded number of samples). *There are absolute constants $C,d_0>0$ with the following property. For $d\ge d_0$, every learner in the stated finite-state model with $M\ge0$ bits and an integer deterministic horizon $T\ge0$ has, for $S\sim\sigma$ independent of its fresh Gaussian rows, $$\label{h:stream:success-bound}
 \mathbb P\{\arccos\langle\widehat s,S\rangle\le\epsilon\}
 \le
 \min\left\{1,
 \left[2\epsilon\exp\!\left(
 C\left(1+\frac{M}{d^2}\right)
 \left\lceil\frac{T}{q}\right\rceil\right)\right]^a
 \right\},$$ where $0<\epsilon\le1/10$ and $a=(d-1)/2$, $q=\lfloor(d-1)/8\rfloor$ as in <a href="#h:local:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:local:parameters">[h:local:parameters]</a>. The probability includes data-independent initialization, the shared seed, and fresh random choices.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-stream-success"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-stream-success</summary>

*Proof.* Use the angular score in Lemma <a href="#h:stream:categories" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:categories">14</a>. Fix a seed and a prior-preserving Borel replacement as in Section <a href="#h:stream:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:reduction">5.2</a>, with prior $\sigma$. If $T=0$, its output is a signal-independent mixture of terminal laws. Lemma <a href="#h:local:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:local:caps">5</a> gives local mass at scale $\epsilon$. The radius-two ball about a sphere point covers $S^{d-1}$, so total success is at most $(2\epsilon)^a$, as required when $\lceil T/q\rceil=0$. We may now suppose $T\ge1$.

Increase the absolute threshold $d_0$ so that $q\ge2$, $q\ge d/16$, and $a\ge d/3$. Partition the $T$ sample indices into $B=\lceil T/q\rceil$ consecutive nonempty blocks, each of length at most $q$. At the final boundary every continuation is a terminal output function and obeys <a href="#h:local:bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:local:bound">[h:local:bound]</a> at scale $\epsilon$. Suppose that at the end of one block, all continuing functions obey that bound at a common scale $R\ge\epsilon$. Its terminal destinations also obey the bound at scale $R$, by Lemma <a href="#h:local:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:local:caps">5</a> and monotonicity in the scale.

For a block of length $m\le q$, Lemma <a href="#h:stream:categories" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:categories">14</a> places every preceding continuation in the form required by Lemma <a href="#h:block:bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:block:bound">13</a>, with $N\le(m+2)2^M\le(q+2)2^M$. That block lemma multiplies the scale by at most $$\exp(C_0d/a)N^{1/(aq)}$$ for an absolute $C_0$. Its logarithm is at most <a id="h:stream:multiplier"></a>$$\label{h:stream:multiplier}
 \frac{C_0d}{a}+
 \frac{M\log2+\log(q+2)}{aq}
 \le C\left(1+\frac{M}{d^2}\right)=:\Lambda,$$ where $d/a\le3$, $1/(aq)\le48/d^2$, and $\log(q+2)/(aq)\le48\log(d+2)/d^2$ allow an absolute $C$. Backward induction gives <a href="#h:local:bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:local:bound">[h:local:bound]</a> at scale $R=\epsilon e^{\Lambda B}$ for every initial state. Averaging over the conditional initial-state law preserves this bound, because that law is independent of the signal and rows. Taking the radius-two ball gives total success at most $(2\epsilon e^{\Lambda B})^a$, and a probability is also at most one.

By the reduction in Section <a href="#h:stream:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:reduction">5.2</a>, this is a uniform bound on the original conditional success probabilities for almost every seed. Averaging them proves <a href="#h:stream:success-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:stream:success-bound">[h:stream:success-bound]</a>; no joint choice of the Borel replacements is used. ◻

</details>

<a id="h:end:section"></a>
### Constant accuracy and the main lower bound

Rounding the horizon up to a whole number of blocks loses less than one block. At fine accuracy this loss is negligible. For the remaining accuracies, even an estimator retaining all the data needs a linear number of equations. Reflection across their row span proves this without identifying the full conditional law of the signal.

<a id="stmt-h-ev-reflection"></a>
<div id="h:ev:reflection" class="lemma">

<a id="h:ev:direct-residual"></a>
<a id="h:ev:antipodal"></a>
<a id="h:ev:reflection-expectation"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Reflection with full data). *Let $d\ge1$ be an integer and $T\ge0$. Let $S$ be uniform on $S^{d-1}$. Let a matrix $A$ with $d$ columns, independent of $S$, have row span $U$ of dimension at most $T$. Suppose an output $\widehat S$ is drawn from a probability kernel depending only on $(A,AS)$. Put $u=P_US$ and $v=(I-P_U)S$. For an arbitrary output in $\mathbb R^d$ and $0<\epsilon<1$, $$\label{h:ev:direct-residual}
 \Pr\{\|\widehat S-S\|\le\epsilon\}
 \le\frac12+\frac{T}{2d(1-\epsilon^2)}.$$ More generally, for $0<b_0<1$ and $0\le\epsilon<\sqrt{1-b_0}$, $$\label{h:ev:antipodal}
 \Pr\{\|\widehat S-S\|\le\epsilon\}
 \le\frac12+\frac12\Pr\{\|u\|^2\ge b_0\}
 \le\frac12+\frac{T}{2b_0d}.$$ For a unit output one also has $$\label{h:ev:reflection-expectation}
 \mathbb E\langle\widehat S,v\rangle=0,\qquad
 \mathbb E\langle\widehat S,S\rangle
 \le\mathbb E\|u\|\le\sqrt{T/d}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-reflection"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-reflection</summary>

*Proof.* The projection $P_U$ is a Borel function of $A$, since $P_U=\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}A^{\mathsf T}(AA^{\mathsf T}+\eta I)^{-1}A$; for a matrix with no rows it is zero. Conditional on $A$, reflection across $U$ sends $S=u+v$ to $u-v$. It preserves uniform spherical measure and the data $(A,AS)$, because $Av=0$, so the output kernel is the same at both signals. If $\|v\|>\epsilon$, the reflected signals have distance greater than $2\epsilon$; a fixed estimate can succeed for at most one of them. Averaging the common output kernel over the reflected pair gives success at most one half on this event. On its complement use the bound one. Therefore $$\Pr\{\|\widehat S-S\|\le\epsilon\}
 \le\frac12+\frac12\Pr\{\|u\|^2\ge1-\epsilon^2\}.$$ Spherical isotropy and independence of $A$ give $$\mathbb E(\|u\|^2\mid A)=\frac{\dim U}{d}\le\frac Td.$$ Markov’s inequality proves <a href="#h:ev:direct-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:direct-residual">[h:ev:direct-residual]</a>. The same pairing on the event $\|u\|^2<b_0$, where $\|v\|>\sqrt{1-b_0}>\epsilon$, proves <a href="#h:ev:antipodal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:antipodal">[h:ev:antipodal]</a>. For unit outputs, reflection cancels $\mathbb E\langle\widehat S,v\rangle$, whereas $\langle\widehat S,u\rangle\le\|u\|$. Cauchy–Schwarz and the same second-moment identity prove <a href="#h:ev:reflection-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:reflection-expectation">[h:ev:reflection-expectation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="h:end:linear-threshold"></a>
For almost every fixed shared seed, use the prior-preserving Borel rules justified in Section <a href="#h:stream:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:section">5</a>. Pre-generate all $T$ Gaussian rows and grant the learner the full matrix and labels. After averaging its signal-independent initial state and fresh choices, its stopped output law is a Borel kernel of those data. Angular error at most $\epsilon$ implies Euclidean error at most $\epsilon$. If $T\le d/4$ and $\epsilon\le1/10$, then <a href="#h:ev:direct-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:direct-residual">[h:ev:direct-residual]</a> gives success probability at most $$\frac12+\frac1{8(1-\epsilon^2)}
 \le\frac{62}{99}<\frac23.$$ This uniform bound also holds for the original conditional success probabilities and hence for their average over the seed. Thus any learner with the stipulated average success must satisfy $$\label{h:end:linear-threshold}
 T>d/4.$$

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-reflection-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-reflection</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#h:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:main">2</a>.* Fix the finite constant $A>0$ in the theorem. Take $d$ above the absolute threshold in Proposition <a href="#h:stream:success" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:success">16</a>, and large enough that $a\ge1$ and $q\ge d/16$. Put $$K_A=\exp(C(1+A))>1,\qquad
 L=\log(1/\epsilon),\qquad B=\lceil T/q\rceil,$$ where $C$ is the absolute constant in that proposition. Since $M\le Ad^2$ and prior success is at least $2/3$, $$\frac23\le(2\epsilon K_A^B)^a.$$ Taking the $a$th root gives $\epsilon K_A^B\ge1/3$. Since $B\le T/q+1$, <a id="h:end:rounding"></a>$$\label{h:end:rounding}
 T\ge q\left(\frac{L-\log3}{\log K_A}-1\right).$$ Let $H_A=2(\log3+\log K_A)$. If $L\ge H_A$, this gives $$T\ge\frac{qL}{2\log K_A}\ge\frac{dL}{32\log K_A}.$$ If $L<H_A$, <a href="#h:end:linear-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:end:linear-threshold">[h:end:linear-threshold]</a> instead gives $T>d/4>dL/(4H_A)$. Thus the same choice $$c_A=\min\left\{\frac1{32\log K_A},\frac1{4H_A}\right\}>0$$ works uniformly over $0<\epsilon\le1/10$ and $M\le Ad^2$ for this fixed $A$. No positive constant uniform as $A$ tends to infinity is asserted. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-reflection-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-reflection</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#h:subquadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:subquadratic">3</a>.* Since $M(d)=o(d^2)$, there is a dimension $d_M$ after which $M(d)\le d^2$. Apply Theorem <a href="#h:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:main">2</a> with the numerical choice $A=1$ at every $d\ge d_M$, so $K_1=e^{2C}$ in the preceding proof. Its constant $c_1$ is absolute, and the eventual dimension threshold may depend on the memory sequence through $d_M$. The argument applies separately at every accuracy $0<\epsilon(d)\le1/10$, so it requires neither monotonicity of that sequence nor a relation between memory and accuracy. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-reflection-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-reflection</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#h:worst-signal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:worst-signal">4</a>.* Average the original fixed-signal experiment over $S\sim\sigma$ before choosing Borel versions. Its measurability makes the resulting success probability the integral of the fixed-signal success probabilities. A success probability at least $2/3$ for every signal therefore gives uniform-prior success at least $2/3$. Theorem <a href="#h:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:main">2</a> and Corollary <a href="#h:subquadratic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:subquadratic">3</a> give the corresponding conclusions with their unchanged constants and dimension thresholds. Lemma <a href="#h:stream:borel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:borel">15</a> preserves the average used in those arguments even if a version changes behavior at exceptional individual signals. ◻

</details>

<a id="h:alternatives"></a>
### Selection on a common data domain

The two applications use the same analytic selection step: several continuation measures are supported on one data domain, and a measurable route chooses among them. The strong density estimate of Theorem <a href="#h:dv:finite-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:finite-density">6</a> is sufficient, but a bound for measurable tests already gives the required $N^{1/p}$ cost.

We state this common step before applying the projection theorem to a lower-dimensional cube prior in Section <a href="#h:cube:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:cube:section">8</a>. The later sections examine additional exact-label interfaces and alternative coefficient normalizations; they do not assert a stronger main theorem.

<a id="stmt-h-alt-selection"></a>
<div id="h:alt:selection" class="lemma">

<a id="h:alt:test"></a>
<a id="h:alt:selection-bound"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Selection on a common data domain). *Let $(E,\mathcal E,\lambda)$ be a measure space, let $\mathcal D\in\mathcal E$ have finite measure, let $p>1$, let $U\ge0$, and let $N\ge1$ be an integer. Suppose finite nonnegative measures $\eta_1,\ldots,\eta_N$ are supported on $\mathcal D$, and that for every measurable $0\le g\le1$, $$\label{h:alt:test}
 \int g\,d\eta_j
 \le U\left(\int_{\mathcal D}g\,d\lambda\right)^{1-1/p}
 \qquad(1\le j\le N).$$ For measurable routes $g_j\ge0$ with $\sum_jg_j\le1$, $$\label{h:alt:selection-bound}
 \sum_{j=1}^N\int g_j\,d\eta_j
 \le N^{1/p}U\,\lambda(\mathcal D)^{1-1/p}.$$ A sufficient condition for <a href="#h:alt:test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:alt:test">[h:alt:test]</a> is $\eta_j=f_j\lambda$, where $f_j\ge0$ vanishes outside $\mathcal D$ and $\|f_j\|_{L^p(\lambda)}\le U$.*

<a id="h:alt:scale-cancellation"></a>
*In particular, if $r>0$, $m,\beta\in\mathbb R$, and positive constants $H,V,L$ satisfy $$U\le HLr^{\beta-m(1-1/p)},\qquad
 \lambda(\mathcal D)\le Vr^m,$$ then the right side of <a href="#h:alt:selection-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:alt:selection-bound">[h:alt:selection-bound]</a> is at most $$\label{h:alt:scale-cancellation}
 N^{1/p}H V^{1-1/p}Lr^\beta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-alt-selection"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-alt-selection</summary>

*Proof.* Each $g_j$ lies in $[0,1]$. Put $b_j=\int_{\mathcal D}g_j\,d\lambda$. Equation <a href="#h:alt:test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:alt:test">[h:alt:test]</a> and Hölder on the finite index set give $$\sum_j\int g_j\,d\eta_j
 \le U\sum_jb_j^{1-1/p}
 \le U N^{1/p}\left(\sum_jb_j\right)^{1-1/p}.$$ Since $\sum_jg_j\le1$, we have $\sum_jb_j\le\lambda(\mathcal D)$, proving <a href="#h:alt:selection-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:alt:selection-bound">[h:alt:selection-bound]</a>. Under the density condition, Hölder gives $$\int g\,d\eta_j
 \le U\left(\int_{\mathcal D}g^{p/(p-1)}\,d\lambda\right)^{1-1/p}
 \le U\left(\int_{\mathcal D}g\,d\lambda\right)^{1-1/p},$$ because $0\le g\le1$. Finally the powers of $r$ in the two assumed bounds add to $\beta$, giving <a href="#h:alt:scale-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:alt:scale-cancellation">[h:alt:scale-cancellation]</a>. ◻

</details>

The test formulation accommodates a direct open-set limit even when one chooses not to infer a strong $L^p$ density from that limit. The density formulation applies to the other passages. In both cases, the reference measure and the common support must be the ones specified by the corresponding projection estimate.

<a id="h:cube:section"></a>
### A paired-coordinate cube application

The exact-density theorem for finite measures also applies to a prior supported on a lower-dimensional subset of the sphere. We illustrate this with a cube embedded by coordinate pairs. Its first coordinates transfer the local mass bound to ambient Euclidean balls, and its Lipschitz bound supplies a common support for the projected labels. These are the hypotheses needed by the general projection and selection estimates.

The resulting success bound concerns the cube prior. It gives a sample lower bound at fine accuracy from cube-average success alone. To cover the remaining accuracies for a learner that succeeds at every unit signal, we use the uniform-sphere endpoint from the main proof.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The prior and its terminal mass

Let $d\ge16$ be an integer, set $n=\lfloor d/2\rfloor$, and give $$Q=[-1/2,1/2]^n$$ Lebesgue probability measure $du$. The cube has volume one. Define <a id="h:cube:map"></a>$$\label{h:cube:map}
 \iota(u)=\frac1{\sqrt n}
 \bigl(u_1,\ldots,u_n,\sqrt{1-u_1^2},\ldots,
       \sqrt{1-u_n^2},0,\ldots,0\bigr)\in\mathbb R^d,$$ with $d-2n$ zero coordinates. Each coordinate pair contributes $1/n$ to the squared norm, so $\iota(Q)\subset S^{d-1}$. The signal prior is $\iota_\#du$.

<a id="h:cube:lipschitz"></a>
Write $\operatorname{pr}:\mathbb R^d\to\mathbb R^n$ for projection onto the first $n$ coordinates. Then $\operatorname{pr}\iota(u)=u/\sqrt n$. On $[-1/2,1/2]$, the derivative of $a\mapsto\sqrt{1-a^2}$ has absolute value at most $1/\sqrt3$. The mean value theorem therefore gives $$\label{h:cube:lipschitz}
 \|\iota(u)-\iota(v)\|\le\frac2{\sqrt n}\|u-v\|
 \qquad(u,v\in Q).$$

<a id="h:cube:local"></a>
Set <a id="h:cube:parameters"></a>$$\label{h:cube:parameters}
 \alpha=\frac n4,\qquad \beta=n-\alpha=\frac{3n}{4},
 \qquad p=\left\lfloor\frac n4\right\rfloor\ge2.$$ For $z\in\mathbb R^n$ and $r>0$, abbreviate $$\mathcal B(z,r)=Q\cap B_n(z,\sqrt n\,r),$$ where $B_j(x,t)$ is the closed Euclidean ball in $\mathbb R^j$. The local bound for a function $f:Q\to[0,1]$ will be $$\label{h:cube:local}
 \int_{\mathcal B(z,r)}f(u)\,du\le Lr^\beta
 \qquad(z\in\mathbb R^n,\ r>0).$$ We will propagate the coefficient $L$ backward through the stream.

<a id="h:cube:terminal"></a>
Fix a terminal state and stopping index, after fixing the independent rule seed. Its output law is independent of the signal. Let $f_{\rm term}(u)$ be the probability that this output has angular error at most $\epsilon$ at $\iota(u)$. For a fixed unit output $\widehat s$, angular error at most $\epsilon$ implies chordal error at most $\epsilon$. Projection onto the first coordinates then gives $$\|u-\sqrt n\,\operatorname{pr}\widehat s\|
 \le\sqrt n\,\epsilon.$$ Let $\omega_j$ be the volume of the unit ball in $\mathbb R^j$. The Gaussian integral gives $\omega_j j^{j/2}\le(2\pi e)^{j/2}$: integrate $e^{-\|x\|^2/2}$ over the ball of radius $\sqrt j$ and compare with its integral over $\mathbb R^j$. The successful part of $\mathcal B(z,r)$ lies in two parameter balls, so its volume is at most the smaller of their volumes. With $C_0=\sqrt{2\pi e}$, the terminal success function consequently satisfies $$\label{h:cube:terminal}
 \int_{\mathcal B(z,r)}f_{\rm term}(u)\,du
 \le C_0^n\min(r,\epsilon)^n
 \le C_0^n\epsilon^\alpha r^\beta.$$ The last inequality uses $\alpha+\beta=n$; the same estimate for a random output follows by integration against its signal-independent law.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Transferring local mass to the embedded measure

Let $\gamma_k$ be the law of a $k$-by-$d$ matrix with independent standard Gaussian entries, and put $$\lambda_k(dA,dy)=\gamma_k(dA)\,dy.$$ The following specialization supplies both the exact density and its common support. A parameter function measurable in the Lebesgue completion can first be replaced by a Borel version under $du$, without changing any local integral.

<a id="stmt-h-cube-density"></a>
<div id="h:cube:density" class="lemma">

<a id="h:cube:joint-measure"></a>
<a id="h:cube:density-bound"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Exact density and a common projected ellipsoid). *Let $k$ be an integer with $1\le k\le p$, and let a Borel function $f:Q\to[0,1]$ satisfy <a href="#h:cube:local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:local">[h:cube:local]</a> with $L\ge0$. For $z\in\mathbb R^n$ and $r>0$, define the finite joint measure $$\label{h:cube:joint-measure}
 \eta_{f,z,r}(E)
 =\int\!\int_{\mathcal B(z,r)}
       \mathbf 1_E(A,A\iota(u))f(u)\,du\,\gamma_k(dA)$$ for Borel $E\subseteq\mathbb R^{k\times d}\times\mathbb R^k$. There is a universal $C_2\ge1$ such that $\eta_{f,z,r}=h_{f,z,r}\lambda_k$ for a nonnegative density with $$\label{h:cube:density-bound}
 \|h_{f,z,r}\|_{L^p(\lambda_k)}
 \le C_2^n Lr^{\beta-k(1-1/p)}.$$ If $\mathcal B(z,r)\ne\varnothing$, fix any $u_*\in\mathcal B(z,r)$ and set <a id="h:cube:ellipsoid-support"></a>$$\label{h:cube:ellipsoid-support}
 \mathcal E_{u_*,r}
 =\{(A,y):y\in A\iota(u_*)+A B_d(0,4r)\}.$$ Every such density, for this same $z,r,u_*$, vanishes almost everywhere outside $\mathcal E_{u_*,r}$, and <a id="h:cube:ellipsoid-volume"></a>$$\label{h:cube:ellipsoid-volume}
 \lambda_k(\mathcal E_{u_*,r})\le C_2^n r^k.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-cube-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-cube-density</summary>

<a id="h:cube:ambient-growth"></a>
*Proof.* An empty $\mathcal B(z,r)$ gives the zero measure. Otherwise fix $u_*\in\mathcal B(z,r)$, and let $$\mu=\iota_\#\bigl(\mathbf1_{\mathcal B(z,r)}f(u)\,du\bigr).$$ For any $x\in\mathbb R^d$ and $t>0$, projection onto the first coordinates gives $$\label{h:cube:ambient-growth}
 \begin{split}
 \mu(B_d(x,t))
 &\le\int_{\mathcal B(\sqrt n\,\operatorname{pr}x,t)}f(u)\,du\\
 &\le Lt^\beta.
 \end{split}$$ For $u\in\mathcal B(z,r)$, the distance from $u$ to $u_*$ is at most $2\sqrt n\,r$. Thus <a href="#h:cube:lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:lipschitz">[h:cube:lipschitz]</a> puts $\mu$ inside $B_d(\iota(u_*),4r)$.

<a id="h:cube:margin"></a>
If $L=0$, <a href="#h:cube:local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:local">[h:cube:local]</a> applied to a ball containing $Q$ gives $f=0$ almost everywhere, and the zero density suffices. Suppose now that $L>0$. Apply the general finite-measure result Theorem <a href="#h:dv:finite-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:finite-density">6</a> with $$H=L,\qquad R=4r,\qquad m=k,
 \qquad \beta=3n/4,\qquad p=\lfloor n/4\rfloor.$$ For its factors $J_j$, the required margin and their uniform bounds are $$\label{h:cube:margin}
 \beta-j-k\ge\frac{3n}{4}-2p+2\ge\frac n4+2,
 \qquad
 J_j=1+3^j2^\beta\frac{k}{\beta-j-k}
 \le1+3^{n/4}2^{3n/4}
 \qquad(0\le j\le p-2).$$ In particular $\beta>k+p-2$. We use the general parameter range of that theorem, not the specialization to exponents near half the ambient dimension. Its exact norm bound is $$(2\pi)^{-k(p-1)/(2p)}
 L(4r)^{\beta-k(1-1/p)}
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{p-2}J_j\right)^{1/p}.$$ The first factor is at most one, each $J_j\le C^n$, and $0<\beta-k(1-1/p)\le\beta$. Absorbing $4^{\beta-k(1-1/p)}\le4^\beta$ into $C_2^n$ proves <a href="#h:cube:density-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:density-bound">[h:cube:density-bound]</a>. This is the density of the exact joint measure <a href="#h:cube:joint-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:joint-measure">[h:cube:joint-measure]</a>; no density relative to spherical area is assumed.

The set $\mathcal E_{u_*,r}$ is the exact projected image of the enclosing ball $B_d(\iota(u_*),4r)$. Lemma <a href="#h:ev:ellipsoid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:ellipsoid">12</a> makes this set Borel and gives the asserted common support. The same lemma gives $$\lambda_k(\mathcal E_{u_*,r})
 \le C_{\mathrm E}^d(4r)^k\le C_2^n r^k,$$ after one universal increase of $C_2$, since $d\le2n+1$ and $k\le n/4$. This calculation also covers the zero-density case. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The block bound and the streaming consequence

The power of $r$ lost in the density norm is restored by the common support volume. Selection then costs only the $p$th root of the number of continuations.

<a id="stmt-h-cube-block"></a>
<div id="h:cube:block" class="corollary">

**Corollary 20** (A block on the paired-coordinate prior). *Let $k,N$ be integers with $1\le k\le p$ and $N\ge1$, and let Borel $f_1,\ldots,f_N:Q\to[0,1]$ satisfy <a href="#h:cube:local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:local">[h:cube:local]</a> with the same $L\ge0$. Suppose Borel functions $g_v:\mathbb R^{k\times d}\times\mathbb R^k\to[0,\infty)$ obey $\sum_{v=1}^N g_v(A,y)\le1$. Then $$F(u)=\mathbb E_{A\sim\gamma_k}
       \sum_{v=1}^N g_v(A,A\iota(u))f_v(u)$$ satisfies <a href="#h:cube:local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:local">[h:cube:local]</a> with $C_3^nN^{1/p}L$ in place of $L$, for a universal $C_3\ge1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-cube-block"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-cube-block</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $z,r$. An empty $\mathcal B(z,r)$ is immediate. Otherwise choose the same $u_*\in\mathcal B(z,r)$ for all $v$. The exact joint densities from Lemma <a href="#h:cube:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:cube:density">19</a> share its region $\mathcal E_{u_*,r}$. Apply Lemma <a href="#h:alt:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:alt:selection">18</a> with density norm at most $C_2^nLr^{\beta-k(1-1/p)}$ and domain measure at most $C_2^nr^k$. The image-measure identity and that lemma give <a id="h:cube:selection"></a>$$\label{h:cube:selection}
 \int_{\mathcal B(z,r)}F(u)\,du
 \le N^{1/p}C_2^n(C_2^n)^{1-1/p}Lr^\beta
 \le C_3^nN^{1/p}Lr^\beta.$$ The radius powers cancel, and the same $C_3$ works for every $1\le k\le p$. ◻

</details>

To apply this bound to the model’s completed-measurable rules, first observe that $f=1$ satisfies $$|\mathcal B(z,r)|\le\min\{1,C_0^nr^n\}\le C_0^nr^\beta.$$ Take $k=1$ in Lemma <a href="#h:cube:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:cube:density">19</a> and a parameter ball containing $Q$. The fresh pair $(X,\langle X,\iota(u)\rangle)$, for $u\sim du$, is therefore absolutely continuous relative to $\gamma_1(dx)\,dy$. We may consequently use the seedwise Borel reduction and fresh-state continuations of Section <a href="#h:stream:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:reduction">5.2</a> under this cube prior. As there, the conditional numerical estimates are averaged using the original measurable experiment; no jointly chosen family of Borel replacements is required.

<a id="stmt-h-cube-success"></a>
<div id="h:cube:success" class="proposition">

<a id="h:cube:success-bound"></a>
<a id="h:cube:sample-result"></a>
**Proposition 21** (Precision bound for the cube prior). *There are universal constants $D\ge e$, $d_0\ge16$, and $c>0$ with the following property. Let $d\ge d_0$, $M\ge0$, and $T\ge0$ be integers, with $M\le d^2$ and $0<\epsilon\le1/10$. Every learner in Definition <a href="#h:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:model">1</a> with these memory and sample bounds has angular success probability under $s=\iota(u)$, $u\sim du$, at most $$\label{h:cube:success-bound}
 \min\left\{1,\,
       D^{n(m+1)}\epsilon^{n/4}\right\},
 \qquad
 m=\left\lceil\frac{T}{k_0}\right\rceil,\qquad
 k_0=\left\lfloor\frac n4\right\rfloor.$$ The probability averages over $u$, the fresh Gaussian samples, and the learner’s randomness. If that same learner has angular success probability at least $2/3$ for every $s\in S^{d-1}$, then $$\label{h:cube:sample-result}
 T\ge c\,d\log(1/\epsilon).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-cube-success"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-cube-success</summary>

*Proof.* Use the seedwise convention just established, and first start from any prescribed initial state. For $T>0$, partition the stream into $m=\lceil T/p\rceil$ consecutive blocks, each of length at most $p=k_0$. Lemma <a href="#h:stream:categories" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:categories">14</a>, with angular terminal score and signal $\iota(u)$, represents the preceding success function by the exact block formula in Corollary <a href="#h:cube:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:cube:block">20</a>. For a block of length $k$, its Borel routes have at most <a id="h:cube:destinations"></a>$$\label{h:cube:destinations}
 N\le(k+2)2^M$$ destinations. The stopping offsets are local to this block, including an immediate stop; its continuing functions use fresh future samples. These are the same phase and terminal-state conventions as in that lemma.

For $n\ge8$, $p\ge n/8$, $d\le2n+1$, and $M\le d^2$, $$\frac{\log N}{p}
 \le\frac{d^2\log2+\log(p+2)}p\le C_4n$$ with a universal $C_4$. Choose a universal $D\ge e$ so that $D\ge C_0$ and $C_3^nN^{1/p}\le D^n$. The terminal coefficient is $C_0^n\epsilon^{n/4}$ by <a href="#h:cube:terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:terminal">[h:cube:terminal]</a>. Backward induction, applying the block bound and retaining the terminal estimate at earlier stops, gives coefficient $$L=D^{n(m+1)}\epsilon^{n/4}$$ for every initial state. The ball $\mathcal B(0,1)$ contains $Q$, so this coefficient bounds total cube-average success. For $T=0$, the terminal bound gives the same statement with $m=0$. All constants are uniform over the seedwise Borel rules and prescribed states. The independent conditional initialization and the averaging of original conditional success probabilities from Section <a href="#h:stream:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:reduction">5.2</a> therefore prove <a href="#h:cube:success-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:success-bound">[h:cube:success-bound]</a> for the original learner, including every short last block.

Suppose now only that the cube-average success is at least $2/3$. Taking natural logarithms in <a href="#h:cube:success-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:success-bound">[h:cube:success-bound]</a> and using $m\le T/k_0+1$ gives <a id="h:cube:sample-bound"></a>$$\label{h:cube:sample-bound}
 \frac{T}{k_0}
 \ge\frac{\log(1/\epsilon)}{4\log D}
       -2-\frac{\log(3/2)}{n\log D}.$$ If $\log(1/\epsilon)\ge24\log D$, its right side is at least $\log(1/\epsilon)/(8\log D)$. Since $k_0\ge n/8$ and $n\ge d/3$, this already gives $T\ge d\log(1/\epsilon)/(192\log D)$ in that accuracy range.

For the all-accuracy conclusion <a href="#h:cube:sample-result" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:sample-result">[h:cube:sample-result]</a>, assume the stated every-signal guarantee. It gives the cube-average premise just used. It also gives uniform-sphere average success at least $2/3$ for the same original learner. The reflection consequence <a href="#h:end:linear-threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:end:linear-threshold">[h:end:linear-threshold]</a> therefore forces $T>d/4$. For $\log(1/\epsilon)<24\log D$, this implies $$T>\frac d4>\frac{d\log(1/\epsilon)}{96\log D}.$$ Combining the two ranges proves <a href="#h:cube:sample-result" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:sample-result">[h:cube:sample-result]</a>, for example with $c=1/(192\log D)$, after increasing the universal dimension threshold as needed. The memory condition $M\le d^2$ in particular holds eventually for every sequence $M(d)=o(d^2)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### An affine-distance interpretation

<a id="h:cube:affine"></a>
The application is complete. The embedding also gives a direct interpretation of the Gram moment behind its projection bound. This alternative calculation isolates what the first coordinates guarantee about affine spans. For $u,u_1,\ldots,u_j\in Q$, $j\ge1$, $$\label{h:cube:affine}
 \operatorname{dist}\bigl(\iota(u),
       \operatorname{aff}(\iota(u_1),\ldots,\iota(u_j))\bigr)
 \ge\frac1{\sqrt n}
       \operatorname{dist}\bigl(u,\operatorname{aff}(u_1,\ldots,u_j)\bigr).$$ Indeed, for $\sum_i c_i=1$, projection of $\iota(u)-\sum_i c_i\iota(u_i)$ is $(u-\sum_i c_i u_i)/\sqrt n$. Projection cannot increase norm, and taking the infimum proves the claim, including dependent points.

<a id="stmt-h-cube-inverse"></a>
<div id="h:cube:inverse" class="lemma">

<a id="h:cube:inverse-bound"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Affine inverse moments). *Let $f:Q\to[0,1]$ be Borel and satisfy <a href="#h:cube:local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:local">[h:cube:local]</a> with $L\ge0$. Let $k$ be an integer with $1\le k\le p$, and let $V\subseteq\mathbb R^n$ be a nonempty affine subspace of dimension $j\le p-2$. There is a universal $C_1\ge1$ such that, for every $z\in\mathbb R^n$, $r>0$, and $0<t\le1$, <a id="h:cube:tube"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\mathcal B(z,r)}f(u)
   \mathbf1_{\{\operatorname{dist}(u,V)\le\sqrt n\,tr\}}\,du
 &\le C_1^n Lr^\beta t^{\beta-j},
 \label{h:cube:tube}\\
 \int_{\mathcal B(z,r)}f(u)
   \left(\frac{\operatorname{dist}(u,V)}{\sqrt n}\right)^{-k}\,du
 &\le C_1^n Lr^{\beta-k}.
 \label{h:cube:inverse-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ The second integrand is nonnegative and extended-valued on $V$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-cube-inverse"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-cube-inverse</summary>

*Proof.* The cases $L=0$ and an empty parameter ball are immediate as above. Apply Proposition <a href="#h:dv:ambient-gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:ambient-gram">7</a> in parameter dimension $n$ to $\mathbf1_{\mathcal B(z,r)}f(u)\,du$, with $$H=Ln^{-\beta/2},\qquad R=\sqrt n\,r,\qquad m=k.$$ The growth hypothesis follows because a parameter ball of radius $a>0$ is $\mathcal B(x,a/\sqrt n)$ after intersection with $Q$. Its tube bound, evaluated at $\sqrt n\,tr$, is $$3^j2^\beta (Ln^{-\beta/2})(\sqrt n\,r)^j
                 (\sqrt n\,tr)^{\beta-j}
 =3^j2^\beta Lr^\beta t^{\beta-j}.$$ Its inverse-distance bound is $J_jLn^{-k/2}r^{\beta-k}$. Multiplication by $n^{k/2}$ gives the normalization in <a href="#h:cube:inverse-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:inverse-bound">[h:cube:inverse-bound]</a>. The bounds <a href="#h:cube:margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:margin">[h:cube:margin]</a> absorb both constants into a universal $C_1^n$. ◻

</details>

For example, let $G$ be the Gram matrix of the $p-1$ differences $\iota(u_i)-\iota(u_1)$, $2\le i\le p$. Almost every parameter tuple is affinely independent, since proper affine subspaces of $\mathbb R^n$ have zero Lebesgue measure. Writing $V_{i-1}=\operatorname{aff}(u_1,\ldots,u_{i-1})$, Gram–Schmidt and <a href="#h:cube:affine" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:affine">[h:cube:affine]</a> give <a id="h:cube:gram"></a>$$\label{h:cube:gram}
 \det G\ge
 \prod_{i=2}^p
 \left(\frac{\operatorname{dist}(u_i,V_{i-1})}{\sqrt n}\right)^2>0.$$ Integrating in the order $u_p,\ldots,u_2$, followed by $u_1$, therefore yields $$\int_{\mathcal B(z,r)^p}\det G^{-k/2}
             \prod_{i=1}^p f(u_i)\,du_1\cdots du_p
 \le C_1^{n(p-1)}L^p r^{\beta p-k(p-1)}.$$ Thus one can also obtain the projection bound by inserting this Gram estimate into the Gaussian difference identity and compact-test passage of Section <a href="#h:projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:projection">3</a>. The main application instead uses the general finite-measure theorem through the ambient ball bound <a href="#h:cube:ambient-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:cube:ambient-growth">[h:cube:ambient-growth]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### An enclosing-ball alternative.

The common ellipsoid may instead be enclosed in the larger label ball <a id="h:cube:support"></a>$$\label{h:cube:support}
 \mathcal R_A=B_k\bigl(A\iota(u_*),4r\|A\|_{\rm op}\bigr).$$ Thus every density in Lemma <a href="#h:cube:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:cube:density">19</a>, with the same $z,r,u_*$, also vanishes almost everywhere outside $\{(A,y):y\in\mathcal R_A\}$. Its volume has the bound <a id="h:cube:volume"></a>$$\label{h:cube:volume}
 \mathbb E_{A\sim\gamma_k}|\mathcal R_A|
 =(4r)^k\mathbb E\,\operatorname{vol}_k B_k(0,\|A\|_{\rm op})
 \le4^k e^{Cd}r^k\le C_2^n r^k,$$ after a universal increase of $C_2$, where $|\mathcal R_A|$ is $k$-dimensional Lebesgue volume. This estimate follows independently from the Gaussian net bound in Lemma <a href="#h:proj:volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:proj:volume">30</a>, not from the smaller ellipsoid’s volume. It gives the same block bound, although the direct proof above uses the exact projected ellipsoid and does not require this enlargement.

<a id="h:dv:section"></a>
### Other exact-label interfaces

Theorem <a href="#h:dv:finite-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:finite-density">6</a> supplies the strong joint $L^p$ density used in both applications. We now separate three additional conclusions that need not be built into that theorem. A direct difference-kernel argument proves the measurable-test estimate needed for selection without using compact-test duality. For spherical restrictions, joint $L^1$ convergence identifies the limiting density; a specified pointwise version works for every full-row-rank matrix, rather than only almost every matrix.

We retain the notation $\eta_\mu,\lambda_m,\mathcal E_{z,R}$ of Section <a href="#h:projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:projection">3</a> and the exact norm scale $\mathcal B$ in <a href="#h:dv:moment-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:moment-scale">[h:dv:moment-scale]</a>. The coefficient $H$ controls mass in all ambient balls, while $R$ is the radius of a supporting ball; neither measure is normalized to a posterior.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### A direct bound for measurable tests

<a id="stmt-h-dv-measurable-test"></a>
<div id="h:dv:measurable-test" class="proposition">

<a id="h:dv:set-bound"></a>
<a id="h:dv:test-bound"></a>
**Proposition 23** (A direct inequality for measurable tests). *Under the assumptions of Proposition <a href="#h:dv:ambient-gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:ambient-gram">7</a>, the exact image measure satisfies $$\eta_\mu(E)\le \mathcal B\,\lambda_m(E)^{1-1/p}
 \label{h:dv:set-bound}$$ for every Borel set $E$ of finite $\lambda_m$-measure. In particular $\eta_\mu\ll\lambda_m$, and the same inequality holds on the $\lambda_m$-completion after extending $\eta_\mu$ to that completion. For every $\lambda_m$-completed-measurable $0\le\varphi\le1$, $$\int\varphi\,d\eta_\mu
 \le\mathcal B
 \left(\int_{\mathcal E_{z,R}}\varphi\,d\lambda_m\right)^{1-1/p}.
 \label{h:dv:test-bound}$$ An infinite integral on the right is allowed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-dv-measurable-test"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-dv-measurable-test</summary>

<a id="h:dv:difference-kernel"></a>
*Proof.* Let $\rho_\delta=(v_m\delta^m)^{-1}
\mathbf1_{B(0,\delta)}$ be the uniform probability density of the radius-$\delta$ ball in $\mathbb R^m$, and set $U_\delta(A,y)=\int\rho_\delta(y-As)\,d\mu(s)$. After translating the common label variable by $As_1$, the tuple kernel in its $p$th moment becomes $$K_\delta(v_2,\ldots,v_p)
 =\int_{\mathbb R^m}\rho_\delta(b)
       \prod_{i=2}^p\rho_\delta(b-v_i)\,db,
 \qquad v_i=A(s_i-s_1).$$ It is nonnegative, and Tonelli’s theorem gives the exact normalization $$\int_{\mathbb R^{m(p-1)}}K_\delta(v_2,\ldots,v_p)
       \,dv_2\cdots dv_p=1.
 \label{h:dv:difference-kernel}$$ In particular the kernel depends on $A$ only through the differences; no independence of $As_1$ from those differences is used.

For a full-rank $V$, the rows of $AV$ are independent centered Gaussian vectors with covariance $V^{\mathsf T}V$. Their joint density has supremum $(2\pi)^{-m(p-1)/2}\det(V^{\mathsf T}V)^{-m/2}$. Multiplying this density by the unit-mass kernel <a href="#h:dv:difference-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:difference-kernel">[h:dv:difference-kernel]</a> bounds the expected tuple kernel by that supremum. Expand $\int U_\delta^p\,d\lambda_m$, discard only the product-null rank exceptions, and apply <a href="#h:dv:gram-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:gram-product">[h:dv:gram-product]</a>. This gives <a id="h:dv:difference-moment"></a>$$\|U_\delta\|_{L^p(\lambda_m)}\le\mathcal B
 \qquad(\delta>0).
 \label{h:dv:difference-moment}$$

The measure $\lambda_m$ is Borel and finite on compact sets in a Euclidean space. The Radon extension theorem (Stanford Mathematics Department, n.d., Corollary 2.11) applies and gives outer regularity on Borel sets. If $O$ is open, then for each $(A,As)\in O$, all sufficiently small label perturbations remain in $O$. Fatou’s lemma followed by Hölder’s inequality gives $$\eta_\mu(O)
 \le\liminf_{\delta\downarrow0}\int_O U_\delta\,d\lambda_m
 \le\mathcal B\,\lambda_m(O)^{1-1/p}.$$ For a Borel $E$ with finite $\lambda_m(E)$, take open supersets whose $\lambda_m$-measures decrease to $\lambda_m(E)$. The last inequality proves <a href="#h:dv:set-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:set-bound">[h:dv:set-bound]</a>. Applied to null sets, it gives $\eta_\mu\ll\lambda_m$. Extending $\eta_\mu$ by zero on $\lambda_m$-null sets gives the asserted version on the $\lambda_m$-completion.

The measure $\eta_\mu$ is supported on the Borel set $\mathcal E_{z,R}$. If $\int_{\mathcal E_{z,R}}\varphi\,d\lambda_m<\infty$, apply <a href="#h:dv:set-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:set-bound">[h:dv:set-bound]</a> to $\{\varphi>t\}\cap\mathcal E_{z,R}$ for $t>0$. Layer cake and concavity of $x^{1-1/p}$ yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \int\varphi\,d\eta_\mu
 &\le\mathcal B\int_0^1
   \lambda_m(\{\varphi>t\}\cap\mathcal E_{z,R})^{1-1/p}\,dt\\
 &\le\mathcal B
   \left(\int_0^1
   \lambda_m(\{\varphi>t\}\cap\mathcal E_{z,R})\,dt\right)^{1-1/p},
\end{aligned}$$ which is <a href="#h:dv:test-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:test-bound">[h:dv:test-bound]</a>. The case of an infinite right side is immediate. This proof supplies the measurable-test estimate directly; it does not infer a strong $L^p$ bound from the set inequality alone. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Spherical absolute continuity and compatible fibers

The remaining constructions use an additional hypothesis: $\mu\ll\sigma_d$ and $m<d$. It provides absolute continuity for each full-row-rank matrix separately. This hypothesis is not needed by the general finite-measure theorem or the preceding direct-test bound.

<a id="stmt-h-dv-gaussian-ac"></a>
<div id="h:dv:gaussian-ac" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Absolute continuity of spherical projections). *Let $d\ge2$ and $1\le m<d$. If $A:\mathbb R^d\to\mathbb R^m$ has full row rank, then $A_\#\sigma_d$ is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. The same holds for $A_\#\mu$ whenever $\mu$ is a finite Borel measure with $\mu\ll\sigma_d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-dv-gaussian-ac"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-dv-gaussian-ac</summary>

*Proof.* Rotate the row space of $A$ to the first $m$ coordinates. The restriction of $A$ to that row space is an invertible map onto $\mathbb R^m$, so it suffices to consider the orthogonal projection onto those coordinates. Write a uniform sphere point as $$\frac{(Z,W)}{\sqrt{\|Z\|^2+\|W\|^2}},$$ where $Z$ and $W$ are independent standard Gaussian vectors in $\mathbb R^m$ and $\mathbb R^{d-m}$. Almost surely $c=\|W\|>0$. Conditional on $c$, the projection is the image of $Z$ under $$z\longmapsto\frac{z}{\sqrt{\|z\|^2+c^2}}.$$ This is a smooth diffeomorphism from $\mathbb R^m$ onto the open unit ball; its inverse is $u\mapsto cu/\sqrt{1-\|u\|^2}$. Thus the conditional projection law has a Lebesgue density, and integrating in $c$ preserves absolute continuity. Applying the invertible row-space map proves the assertion for $A_\#\sigma_d$. Absolute continuity of $A_\#\mu$ follows from $\mu\ll\sigma_d$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-dv-gaussian-ac-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-dv-gaussian-ac</summary>

*A second proof by hemisphere graphs.* <span id="h:dv:hemisphere-ac" label="h:dv:hemisphere-ac"></span> The two open hemispheres of $S^{d-1}$ are the graphs $$x\longmapsto\bigl(x,\pm\sqrt{1-\|x\|^2}\bigr),
 \qquad \|x\|<1,\quad x\in\mathbb R^{d-1}.$$ Their surface-area element is $(1-\|x\|^2)^{-1/2}dx$, and the omitted equator has surface measure zero. Projection of $\sigma_d$ onto the first $d-1$ coordinates therefore has a Lebesgue density. Integrating that density in the last $d-1-m$ coordinates proves the same for projection onto the first $m$ coordinates. Rotation, the invertible row-space map, and $\mu\ll\sigma_d$ finish the argument as in the first proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-h-dv-joint-density"></a>
<div id="h:dv:joint-density" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (A joint density and its fibers). *Let $d\ge2$, $1\le m<d$, and let $\mu$ be a finite nonnegative Borel measure with $\mu\ll\sigma_d$. Then $\eta_\mu$ has a nonnegative jointly Borel density $F(A,y)$ with respect to $\lambda_m$. For $\gamma_m$-almost every $A$, $F(A,\cdot)$ is a density of $A_\#\mu$.*

*If $\mu$ is supported on $B(z,R)$, the density can be chosen to vanish outside the Borel set $\mathcal E_{z,R}\subseteq\mathcal S_{z,R}$ defined in <a href="#h:dv:support-sets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:support-sets">[h:dv:support-sets]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-dv-joint-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-dv-joint-density</summary>

*Proof.* For a Borel set $E$ in the matrix–label space, put $E_A=\{y:(A,y)\in E\}$. If $\lambda_m(E)=0$, Fubini’s theorem gives $|E_A|=0$ for $\gamma_m$-almost every $A$. Such a matrix has full row rank almost surely, and Lemma <a href="#h:dv:gaussian-ac" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:gaussian-ac">24</a> therefore gives $(A_\#\mu)(E_A)=0$. A second application of Fubini shows that $\eta_\mu(E)=0$. Thus the finite measure $\eta_\mu$ is absolutely continuous with respect to the $\sigma$-finite measure $\lambda_m$. The ordinary Radon–Nikodym theorem (Hunter 2011, Theorem 6.27) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[HunterMeasure2011](#ref-HunterMeasure2011)], applied on the product Borel $\sigma$-algebra, gives a nonnegative jointly Borel density $F$.

We verify that one common full-measure set of matrices has the asserted fiber property. Let $\mathcal C$ be the countable $\pi$-system of half-open boxes with rational endpoints, the empty set, and $\mathbb R^m$ itself. It generates the Borel sets of $\mathbb R^m$. For $C\in\mathcal C$ and every Borel matrix set $D$, the density identity applied to $D\times C$ gives $$\int_D(A_\#\mu)(C)\,d\gamma_m(A)
 =
 \int_D\int_C F(A,y)\,dy\,d\gamma_m(A).$$ Both integrands are measurable functions of $A$. Since this equality holds for every $D$, they agree almost everywhere. Remove the union of these exceptional sets over the countable class $\mathcal C$. For every remaining $A$, the two finite measures agree on $\mathcal C$, and hence on every Borel set by the uniqueness theorem for measures. This proves the fiber assertion.

When $\mu$ is supported on $B(z,R)$, its image is carried by the Borel set $\mathcal E_{z,R}$. Multiplying $F$ by the indicator of that set preserves the density identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Convergence and an everywhere-full-rank version

The next result asserts actual convergence in the joint $L^1$ space. The final proposition specifies a single Borel version whose fiber is a density for every full-row-rank matrix, with a separate Lebesgue-null exceptional set of labels allowed for each matrix.

<a id="stmt-h-dv-joint-l1"></a>
<div id="h:dv:joint-l1" class="proposition">

**Proposition 26** (Joint $L^1$ convergence). *In addition to the assumptions of Proposition <a href="#h:dv:ambient-gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:ambient-gram">7</a>, suppose that $d\ge2$, $1\le m<d$, and $\mu\ll\sigma_d$. Let $F$ be the joint density from Lemma <a href="#h:dv:joint-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:joint-density">25</a>, and let $F_\delta$ be the Gaussian regularization in Lemma <a href="#h:dv:gaussian-moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:gaussian-moment">9</a>. Then <a id="h:dv:joint-l1-bound"></a>$$\|F_\delta-F\|_{L^1(\lambda_m)}\longrightarrow0
 \quad\hbox{as }\delta\downarrow0,
 \qquad
 \|F\|_{L^p(\lambda_m)}\le\mathcal B.
 \label{h:dv:joint-l1-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-dv-joint-l1"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-dv-joint-l1</summary>

*Proof.* For almost every $A$, the function $F_A=F(A,\cdot)$ is a nonnegative $L^1$ density of $A_\#\mu$, of mass $\mu(\mathbb R^d)$, and $F_\delta(A,\cdot)=F_A*\phi_\delta$. Tao’s continuity theorem for translations in $L^1(\mathbb R^m)$ (Tao 2011, Proposition 1.6.13) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[TaoMeasure2011](#ref-TaoMeasure2011)] gives $$\|F_A*\phi_\delta-F_A\|_1
 \le\int\phi_\delta(z)\,
          \|F_A(\,\cdot-z)-F_A\|_1\,dz
 \longrightarrow0.$$ For completeness, fix a small translation radius on which the translation norm is small. The integral inside that radius is then small, and the remaining part is at most $2\|F_A\|_1$ times the Gaussian mass outside that radius, which tends to zero. This proves the displayed limit.

The fiber difference norm is at most $2\mu(\mathbb R^d)$. Because $F$ and $F_\delta$ are jointly measurable, these norms are measurable functions of $A$. Dominated convergence in $\gamma_m$ therefore proves the joint $L^1$ limit. Choose a sequence $\delta_i\downarrow0$ for which $\sum_i\|F_{\delta_i}-F\|_{L^1(\lambda_m)}<\infty$. Tonelli’s theorem implies $F_{\delta_i}\to F$ almost everywhere. Fatou’s lemma and <a href="#h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound">[h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound]</a> then give $\int F^p\,d\lambda_m\le\mathcal B^p$. The density identity from Lemma <a href="#h:dv:joint-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:joint-density">25</a> now applies to arbitrary bounded $\lambda_m$-completed-measurable functions of the exact matrix–label pair. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-h-dv-pointwise-density"></a>
<div id="h:dv:pointwise-density" class="proposition">

<a id="h:dv:pointwise-version"></a>
<a id="h:dv:pointwise-moment"></a>
**Proposition 27** (A pointwise joint density version). *Assume the same hypotheses as in Proposition <a href="#h:dv:joint-l1" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:joint-l1">26</a>. For the Gaussian density $\phi_\delta$, define <a id="h:dv:radial-mixture"></a>$$w_\delta(r)
 =v_m r^m\frac{r}{\delta^2}\phi_\delta(r e_1),
 \qquad r>0,
 \label{h:dv:radial-mixture}$$ where $e_1$ is a unit coordinate vector. This is a probability density on $(0,\infty)$, and $\phi_\delta$ is the corresponding mixture of uniform ball densities. Define $$F^\sharp(A,y)=
 \begin{cases}
  \displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}F_{1/i}(A,y),
       &\text{if this limit exists and is finite},\\
  0,   &\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{h:dv:pointwise-version}$$ Then $F^\sharp$ is jointly Borel. For every full-row-rank $A$, $F^\sharp(A,\cdot)$ is a density of $A_\#\mu$ up to a Lebesgue null set, and it is a density of $\eta_\mu$ with respect to $\lambda_m$. Moreover $$\int(F^\sharp)^p\,d\lambda_m\le\mathcal B^p.
 \label{h:dv:pointwise-moment}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-dv-pointwise-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-dv-pointwise-density</summary>

*Proof.* The negative radial derivative of the Gaussian is $-\frac{d}{dr}\phi_\delta(re_1)
=(r/\delta^2)\phi_\delta(re_1)$. Hence $$\int_{\|y\|}^{\infty}\frac{w_\delta(r)}{v_mr^m}\,dr
 =\phi_\delta(y).$$ This is precisely the claimed ball mixture. Integrating it in $y$ and using Tonelli proves $\int_0^\infty w_\delta(r)\,dr=1$. Also $w_\delta(r)=\delta^{-1}w_1(r/\delta)$; its probability mass therefore concentrates at radius zero as $\delta\downarrow0$.

Fix a full-row-rank $A$. Lemma <a href="#h:dv:gaussian-ac" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:gaussian-ac">24</a> gives a nonnegative $L^1$ density $f_A$ of $A_\#\mu$. At a Lebesgue point $y$ of $f_A$, its ball averages $$a_r(y)=\frac{1}{v_mr^m}\int_{B(y,r)}f_A(x)\,dx$$ tend to $f_A(y)$ as $r\downarrow0$, by the Lebesgue differentiation theorem (Tao 2011, Theorem 1.6.19) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[TaoMeasure2011](#ref-TaoMeasure2011)]. The mixture formula gives $$F_\delta(A,y)
 =(f_A*\phi_\delta)(y)
 =\int_0^\infty w_\delta(r)a_r(y)\,dr.$$ Given an error tolerance, choose $r_0>0$ such that $a_r(y)$ is within that tolerance of $f_A(y)$ for $0<r<r_0$. For $r\ge r_0$, the averages are bounded by $\mu(\mathbb R^d)/(v_mr_0^m)$. The part of the mixture on $[r_0,\infty)$ thus tends to zero after subtracting $f_A(y)$, while the part on $(0,r_0)$ has the chosen error bound. It follows that $F_\delta(A,y)\to f_A(y)$ at every Lebesgue point.

Each $F_{1/i}$ is jointly Borel. The set where its sequence has a finite limit is Borel, so <a href="#h:dv:pointwise-version" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:pointwise-version">[h:dv:pointwise-version]</a> is jointly Borel as well. The preceding fixed-$A$ argument shows that its fiber is a density for every full-row-rank $A$, outside a null set of labels. Full row rank holds $\gamma_m$-almost surely, and Fubini therefore identifies $F^\sharp$ with a joint density of $\eta_\mu$. Finally $(F^\sharp)^p\le\liminf_i F_{1/i}^p$ everywhere by its definition. Fatou’s lemma and <a href="#h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound">[h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound]</a> prove <a href="#h:dv:pointwise-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:pointwise-moment">[h:dv:pointwise-moment]</a>. ◻

</details>

The direct-test argument applies to the arbitrary finite measure in Proposition <a href="#h:dv:ambient-gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:ambient-gram">7</a>. The joint $L^1$ and pointwise constructions use the additional spherical absolute continuity, which holds for every restriction $\mu=\mathbf1_{B(z,R)}h\,\sigma_d$ with $0\le h\le1$. In each case the resulting test or density identity is for exact observations.

<a id="h:ev:section"></a>
### Coefficient recurrences and support alternatives

The main block estimate propagated a scale inside a fixed radius power. An equivalent formulation propagates the coefficient of that power. Keeping the exponent variable makes the different terminal accuracy powers visible, while one recurrence handles all of them. The density and exact ellipsoid estimates have already been proved. We first record their spherical specialization and two optional calculations, then give the common coefficient recurrence.

Write $\sigma=\sigma_d$ and $n=d-1$. For unit vectors $u,v$, put $\operatorname{angle}(u,v)=\arccos\langle u,v\rangle$. The following specialization keeps the local exponent variable while fixing a common moment order.

<a id="stmt-auto-corollary-1"></a>
<div class="corollary">

<a id="h:dv:parameters"></a>
<a id="h:dv:dimension-margin"></a>
**Corollary 28** (The dimension-scale parameter regime). *Under the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#h:dv:ambient-gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:ambient-gram">7</a>, suppose that $$d\ge16,\qquad p=\lfloor d/8\rfloor,\qquad
 1\le m\le p,\qquad \frac{d-1}{2}\le\beta\le\frac d2,
 \label{h:dv:parameters}$$ Then, for every $0\le j\le p-2$, $$\beta-j-m\ge\frac{d-1}{2}-2p+2
 \ge\frac d4+\frac32,
 \qquad J_j\le C^d,
 \label{h:dv:dimension-margin}$$ where $C\ge1$ is an absolute constant. Thus the right side of <a href="#h:dv:gram-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:gram-product">[h:dv:gram-product]</a> is at most $C^{d(p-1)}H^pR^{\beta p-m(p-1)}$, uniformly also for short blocks $m<p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-corollary-1"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-corollary-1</summary>

*Proof.* Under <a href="#h:dv:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:parameters">[h:dv:parameters]</a>, $p\le d/8$ gives the displayed margin. Also $m/(\beta-j-m)\le1/2$, while $3^j2^\beta\le3^{d/8}2^{d/2}$. Enlarging one absolute $C$ gives $J_j\le C^d$, and multiplying over the $p-1$ values of $j$ proves the specialization. ◻

</details>

Here $p\ge d/16$ is the moment order and maximum block length, whereas $m$ is the current number of rows. The constants below are absolute unless a dependence is stated.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Spherical restrictions and a second moment calculation

<a id="stmt-h-ev-density"></a>
<div id="h:ev:density" class="lemma">

<a id="h:ev:density-moment"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Exact projection density from an ambient ball bound). *Assume <a href="#h:dv:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:parameters">[h:dv:parameters]</a>, and let $H,R>0$ and $z\in\mathbb R^d$. Let $f:S^{d-1}\to[0,1]$ be Borel measurable, let $\nu=f\sigma$, and suppose $\nu(B(x,r))\le H r^\beta$ for every $x\in\mathbb R^d$ and $r>0$. For $\mu=\mathbf 1_{B(z,R)}\nu$, the pushforward $A_\#\mu$ has, for almost every $A$, a Lebesgue density $F(A,\cdot)$. There is an absolute $C_{\mathrm D}\ge1$ for which a jointly measurable nonnegative choice of these densities satisfies $$\label{h:ev:density-moment}
 \int F(A,y)^p\,d\lambda_m(A,y)
 \le C_{\mathrm D}^{d(p-1)}
       H^pR^{\beta p-m(p-1)}.$$ The density represents the exact labels $As$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-density</summary>

*Proof.* The restriction $\mu$ is supported on $B(z,R)$ and inherits the all-radius bound. Theorem <a href="#h:dv:finite-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:finite-density">6</a>, together with <a href="#h:dv:dimension-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:dimension-margin">[h:dv:dimension-margin]</a>, gives its strong joint $L^p$ density and <a href="#h:ev:density-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:density-moment">[h:ev:density-moment]</a>. The fiber identification in Lemma <a href="#h:dv:joint-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:joint-density">25</a> gives the asserted common almost-everywhere choice of projection densities. ◻

</details>

The same bound can be obtained from ball averages with a less sharp explicit prefactor. This calculation is independent of the choice of terminal coefficient or support domain.

<a id="h:ev:ball-intersection"></a>
<a id="h:ev:ball-moment"></a>
For $\delta>0$, set $$F^{\mathrm{ball}}_\delta(A,y)
 =\frac{1}{v_m\delta^m}
   \int\mathbf 1_{\{\|As-y\|\le\delta\}}\,d\mu(s).$$ These functions are jointly measurable. For almost every $A$ they are the ball averages of the exact density $F(A,\cdot)$; hence Lebesgue differentiation and Fubini give $F^{\mathrm{ball}}_\delta\to F$ for $\lambda_m$-almost every $(A,y)$ along $\delta=1/k$. In particular this limit, set to zero where it does not exist finitely, is a jointly measurable density version. Expand the $p$th moment and apply Tonelli’s theorem. The intersection of the $p$ balls of radius $\delta$ about $As_1,\ldots,As_p$ has volume at most $v_m\delta^m$, and is empty unless $\|A(s_i-s_1)\|\le2\delta$ for every $i\ge2$. Therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 \int (F^{\mathrm{ball}}_\delta)^p\,d\lambda_m
 &\le (v_m\delta^m)^{-(p-1)}
 \int
 \Pr_A\{\|A(s_i-s_1)\|\le2\delta,\ 2\le i\le p\}
 \,d\mu^{\otimes p}.
 \label{h:ev:ball-intersection}
\end{aligned}$$ For a full-column-rank $V=[s_2-s_1,\ldots,s_p-s_1]$, the rows of $AV$ are independent centered Gaussian vectors with covariance $V^{\mathsf T}V$. Their joint density is bounded above by $$(2\pi)^{-m(p-1)/2}\det(V^{\mathsf T}V)^{-m/2}.$$ In the difference variables $AV$, the event in <a href="#h:ev:ball-intersection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:ball-intersection">[h:ev:ball-intersection]</a> lies in the product of $p-1$ radius-$2\delta$ balls in $\mathbb R^m$, whose $m(p-1)$-dimensional volume is $(v_m(2\delta)^m)^{p-1}$. The exceptional rank-deficient tuples are null by <a href="#h:dv:gram-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:gram-product">[h:dv:gram-product]</a>. It follows that $$\label{h:ev:ball-moment}
 \int (F^{\mathrm{ball}}_\delta)^p\,d\lambda_m
 \le 2^{m(p-1)}(2\pi)^{-m(p-1)/2}
     \int\det(V^{\mathsf T}V)^{-m/2}\,d\mu^{\otimes p}.$$ All factors $v_m\delta^m$ have canceled. Combining <a href="#h:ev:ball-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:ball-moment">[h:ev:ball-moment]</a> with <a href="#h:dv:gram-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:gram-product">[h:dv:gram-product]</a>, absorbing $2^{m(p-1)}$ into an absolute constant to the power $d(p-1)$, and using Fatou’s lemma proves <a href="#h:ev:density-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:density-moment">[h:ev:density-moment]</a>.

For the full-block choice $\beta=(d-1)/2$ and $m=p=\lfloor d/8\rfloor$, <a href="#h:ev:ball-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:ball-moment">[h:ev:ball-moment]</a> retains the factor $2^{p(p-1)}$. The margin in <a href="#h:dv:dimension-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:dimension-margin">[h:dv:dimension-margin]</a> is in particular at least $d/8$. Taking the $p$th root gives the form <a id="h:ev:half-sphere-density"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-sphere-density}
 \|F\|_{L^p(\lambda_p)}
 \le C_{\mathrm{ball}}^d H R^{\beta-p(1-1/p)}.$$

For Gaussian averages no new identity is needed: <a href="#h:proj:gaussian-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:proj:gaussian-identity">[h:proj:gaussian-identity]</a> with $k=p$ and <a href="#h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound">[h:dv:gaussian-moment-bound]</a> give the exact covariance $V^{\mathsf T}V+\delta^2(I_{p-1}+\mathbf1\mathbf1^{\mathsf T})$ and prefactor $(2\pi)^{-m(p-1)/2}$. The compact-test, joint-$L^1$, and pointwise passages all give the same moment estimate under their respective hypotheses. In particular, setting $\beta=d/2$ in <a href="#h:ev:density-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:density-moment">[h:ev:density-moment]</a> gives, for every $1\le m\le p$, <a id="h:ev:half-ambient-density"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-ambient-density}
 \mathbb E_A\int F(A,y)^p\,dy
 \le C_{\mathrm D}^{d(p-1)}
       H^pR^{(d/2)p-m(p-1)}.$$

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Enclosing balls as an alternative support

The exact image domain $\mathcal E_{z,R}$ has the volume in Lemma <a href="#h:ev:ellipsoid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:ellipsoid">12</a>. The larger operator-norm ball $\mathcal S_{z,R}$ from <a href="#h:dv:support-sets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:support-sets">[h:dv:support-sets]</a> also suffices; the following proof records its uniformity for short blocks.

<a id="stmt-h-proj-volume"></a>
<div id="h:proj:volume" class="lemma">

<a id="h:proj:volume-bound"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Projected support volume). *For a standard Gaussian $m$-by-$d$ matrix $A$, where $1\le m\le d$, $$\label{h:proj:volume-bound}
 \mathbb E\,\operatorname{vol}_m B(0,\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}})\le\exp(Cd).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-proj-volume"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-proj-volume</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $1/4$-nets of the unit spheres in $\mathbb R^d$ and $\mathbb R^m$ with at most $9^d$ and $9^m$ points. Approximating both arguments in the bilinear supremum loses at most $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}/2$, so $$\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le2\max_{u,v}|v^{\mathsf T}Au|$$ over the net pairs. Each scalar is standard Gaussian. For such a scalar $Z$, $\mathbb E|Z|^m\le(C\sqrt m)^m$: use $|z|^m e^{-z^2/4}\le(2m/e)^{m/2}$ and $\mathbb Ee^{Z^2/4}=\sqrt2$. Bounding the maximum’s $m$-th power by the sum gives $$\mathbb E\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^m\le2^m9^{m+d}(C\sqrt m)^m.$$ The unit $m$-ball volume $v_m$ obeys $v_m\le(2\pi e/m)^{m/2}$: integrate $e^{-\|y\|^2/2}$ over the ball of radius $\sqrt m$ and compare with its full Gaussian integral. Multiplication cancels $m^{m/2}$, proving <a href="#h:proj:volume-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:proj:volume-bound">[h:proj:volume-bound]</a> uniformly even for a short block. ◻

</details>

<a id="h:dv:enclosing-volume"></a>
Since $\lambda_m(\mathcal S_{z,R})
 =v_mR^m\mathbb E\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^m$, this proves $$\label{h:dv:enclosing-volume}
 \lambda_m(\mathcal S_{z,R})\le C_{\mathrm V}^dR^m
 \qquad(1\le m\le d).$$

There is a second net calculation with a different explicit factor. Maximal $1/2$-separated subsets of the unit spheres in $\mathbb R^d$ and $\mathbb R^m$ are $1/2$-nets of sizes at most $5^d$ and $5^m$. Approximating the supremum of a linear form in each argument in turn gives $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le4\max|v^{\mathsf T}Au|$ over the net pairs. Each scalar in this maximum is a standard Gaussian $Z$, and $$\mathbb E|Z|^m
 \le\sqrt2(2m/e)^{m/2},$$ because $|t|^me^{-t^2/4}\le(2m/e)^{m/2}$ and $\mathbb E e^{Z^2/4}=\sqrt2$. Bounding the maximum’s $m$th power by the sum therefore gives <a id="h:dv:half-net"></a>$$\mathbb E\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^m
 \le4^m5^{d+m}\sqrt2(2m/e)^{m/2}.
 \label{h:dv:half-net}$$ Again multiplying by $v_mR^m$ proves <a href="#h:dv:enclosing-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:enclosing-volume">[h:dv:enclosing-volume]</a>, with a possibly larger absolute constant. This calculation retains the $1/2$-net factor $4$.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Terminal coefficients

The geometric exponent $\beta$ may be chosen before either of the following terminal estimates. The hemisphere proof keeps the explicit coefficient $4^n$; the polar proof records two useful denominator bounds.

<a id="stmt-h-dv-hemisphere-cap"></a>
<div id="h:dv:hemisphere-cap" class="lemma">

<a id="h:dv:hemisphere-ball"></a>
<a id="h:dv:terminal-coefficient"></a>
**Lemma 31** (An ambient ball bound from a hemisphere). *Let $d\ge2$, $n=d-1$, and let $\sigma_d$ be uniform probability on $S^{d-1}$. Then $$\sigma_d(B(z,r))\le(4r)^n
 \qquad(z\in\mathbb R^d,\ r>0).
 \label{h:dv:hemisphere-ball}$$ Let $\epsilon>0$, let $\kappa$ be any probability law on $S^{d-1}$, independent of the target, and put $h(s)=\int\mathbf 1_{\{\|s-u\|\le\epsilon\}}\,\kappa(du)$. For $0<\beta<n$, every $z\in\mathbb R^d$, and every $r>0$, $$\int_{B(z,r)}h\,d\sigma_d
 \le4^n\min\{r^n,\epsilon^n\}
 \le4^n\epsilon^{n-\beta}r^\beta.
 \label{h:dv:terminal-coefficient}$$ The same upper bound holds for the angular-success function, since angular distance at most $\epsilon$ implies chordal distance at most $\epsilon$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-dv-hemisphere-cap"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-dv-hemisphere-cap</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first that $0<r\le1/4$ and that $B(z,r)$ meets the sphere. Choose a point $a$ of the intersection and rotate it to a coordinate axis. Every other point of the intersection is within chordal distance $2r$ of $a$, lies in the corresponding open hemisphere, and projects into the radius-$2r$ ball in $\mathbb R^n$. On that projected ball the graph area element is at most $2$, because $2r\le1/2$. Thus the area of the patch is at most $2v_n(2r)^n$, where $v_n$ is the volume of the unit $n$-ball. The two hemisphere graphs together have area at least $2v_n$. The resulting probability is at most $(2r)^n$, and hence at most $(4r)^n$. An empty intersection is harmless. For $r>1/4$, the bound $\sigma_d(B(z,r))\le1<(4r)^n$ proves <a href="#h:dv:hemisphere-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:hemisphere-ball">[h:dv:hemisphere-ball]</a>.

For fixed $u$, the successful part of $B(z,r)$ is contained both in $B(z,r)$ and in $B(u,\epsilon)$. Apply <a href="#h:dv:hemisphere-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:hemisphere-ball">[h:dv:hemisphere-ball]</a> to each ball, take the smaller bound, and integrate in $\kappa$. Finally $\min\{r^n,\epsilon^n\}\le\epsilon^{n-\beta}r^\beta$, by considering $r\le\epsilon$ and $r\ge\epsilon$ separately. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-h-ev-polar-cap"></a>
<div id="h:ev:polar-cap" class="lemma">

<a id="h:ev:polar-terminal"></a>
**Lemma 32** (Polar cap estimates). *Let $d\ge2$, $n=d-1$, and let $\sigma$ be uniform on $S^{d-1}$. There is an absolute $C_0\ge1$ such that $\sigma(B(z,R))\le C_0^dR^n$ for every ambient center $z$ and every $R>0$. Let $\epsilon>0$. If a unit output is drawn from a law independent of the signal, its success function $h(s)$, for either angular error at most $\epsilon$ or chordal error at most $\epsilon$, satisfies $$\label{h:ev:polar-terminal}
 \int_{B(z,R)}h\,d\sigma
 \le C_0^d\min(R^n,\epsilon^n)
 \le C_0^d\epsilon^{n-\beta}R^\beta
 \qquad(0<\beta<n).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-polar-cap"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-polar-cap</summary>

*Proof.* In spherical polar coordinates, the area element at polar angle $t$ is $\sin^{d-2}t\,dt$ times the area element on $S^{d-2}$. Dividing the cap area by the total area therefore shows that a cap of angular radius $0\le\theta\le\pi$ has mass $$\frac{\int_0^\theta\sin^{d-2}t\,dt}
      {\int_0^\pi\sin^{d-2}t\,dt}.$$ The numerator is at most $\theta^n/n$, since $\sin t\le t$. Restricting the denominator to two different intervals gives <a id="h:ev:polar-denominators"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \int_0^\pi\sin^{d-2}t\,dt
 &\ge \frac\pi3\left(\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\right)^{d-2}
 &&\text{from }[\pi/3,2\pi/3],
 \notag\\
 \int_0^\pi\sin^{d-2}t\,dt
 &\ge \frac{2\pi}{3}\,2^{-(d-2)}
 &&\text{from }[\pi/6,5\pi/6].
 \label{h:ev:polar-denominators}
\end{aligned}$$ Either estimate gives an upper bound $C^d\theta^n$ for the cap.

If $B(z,R)$ meets the sphere, choose $s_0$ in the intersection. Every other point in it has chordal distance at most $2R$ from $s_0$. The angle between two unit vectors is at most $\pi/2$ times their chordal distance. Thus their angle is at most $\pi R$ when $R\le1$; for $R\le1/4$ the bound $2\arcsin R\le4R$ is also available. The cap estimate and the trivial mass bound one for $R\ge1$ give the asserted ambient-ball bound after enlarging $C_0$.

For a fixed unit output, angular error at most $\epsilon$ implies chordal error at most $\epsilon$, because $2\sin(\theta/2)\le\theta$. The global success mass is therefore at most $C_0^d\epsilon^n$, while its mass in $B(z,R)$ is at most the ambient-ball bound $C_0^dR^n$. Averaging these bounds over the output law proves the minimum in <a href="#h:ev:polar-terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:polar-terminal">[h:ev:polar-terminal]</a>. If $R\le\epsilon$, then $R^n\le\epsilon^{n-\beta}R^\beta$; if $R\ge\epsilon$, then $\epsilon^n\le\epsilon^{n-\beta}R^\beta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="h:ev:hemisphere-terminal"></a>
Lemma <a href="#h:dv:hemisphere-cap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:hemisphere-cap">31</a> gives the alternative coefficient $$\label{h:ev:hemisphere-terminal}
 \int_{B(z,R)}h\,d\sigma
 \le4^n\min(R^n,\epsilon^n)
 \le4^n\epsilon^{n-\beta}R^\beta.$$ For $\beta=n/2$, the looser coefficient $L_0=4^d\epsilon^\beta$ follows immediately from <a href="#h:ev:hemisphere-terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:hemisphere-terminal">[h:ev:hemisphere-terminal]</a>. For $\beta=d/2$, <a href="#h:ev:polar-terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:polar-terminal">[h:ev:polar-terminal]</a> gives <a id="h:ev:half-ambient-terminal"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-ambient-terminal}
 H_0=C_0^d\epsilon^{d-1-d/2}
     =C_0^d\epsilon^{d/2-1}.$$

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### One coefficient recurrence

<a id="stmt-h-ev-coefficient-step"></a>
<div id="h:ev:coefficient-step" class="proposition">

**Proposition 33** (Backward coefficient step). *Assume <a href="#h:dv:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:parameters">[h:dv:parameters]</a>. Let $H>0$ and let $N\ge1$ be an integer. Let $h_1,\ldots,h_N:S^{d-1}\to[0,1]$ be Borel functions satisfying $$\int_{B(x,r)}h_w\,d\sigma\le H r^\beta
 \qquad(x\in\mathbb R^d,\ r>0,\ 1\le w\le N).$$ Let $g_w(A,y)\ge0$ be Borel functions with $\sum_wg_w\le1$, and define $$h(s)=\mathbb E_A\sum_{w=1}^N g_w(A,As)h_w(s).$$ There is an absolute $C_{\mathrm{step}}\ge1$ such that <a id="h:ev:coefficient-bound"></a>$$\label{h:ev:coefficient-bound}
 \int_{B(z,R)}h\,d\sigma
 \le C_{\mathrm{step}}^dN^{1/p}H R^\beta
 \qquad(z\in\mathbb R^d,\ R>0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-coefficient-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-coefficient-step</summary>

*Proof.* For the fixed ball $B(z,R)$, take $\mu_w=\mathbf1_{B(z,R)}h_w\sigma$ in Lemma <a href="#h:ev:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:density">29</a>. Its exact density $F_w$ is supported on the common domain $\mathcal E_{z,R}$ of Lemma <a href="#h:ev:ellipsoid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:ellipsoid">12</a>. The image identity gives $$\int_{B(z,R)}h\,d\sigma
 =\int_{\mathcal E_{z,R}}\sum_w g_w(A,y)F_w(A,y)
       \,d\lambda_m(A,y).$$ The density condition of Lemma <a href="#h:alt:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:alt:selection">18</a> applies on this domain with its norm parameter equal to $$\mathcal U=C_{\mathrm D}^{d(1-1/p)}
             H R^{\beta-m(1-1/p)}.$$ Its conclusion and Lemma <a href="#h:ev:ellipsoid" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:ellipsoid">12</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{B(z,R)}h\,d\sigma
 &\le N^{1/p}\mathcal U
       \lambda_m(\mathcal E_{z,R})^{1-1/p}\\
 &\le N^{1/p}C_{\mathrm D}^{d(1-1/p)}
       H R^{\beta-m(1-1/p)}(C_{\mathrm E}^dR^m)^{1-1/p}\\
 &\le C_{\mathrm{step}}^dN^{1/p}H R^\beta.
