\end{aligned}$$ The maximum-density proof of the same estimate observes pointwise that $\sum_wg_wF_w\le\max_wF_w$ and $(\max_wF_w)^p\le\sum_wF_w^p$, then applies Hölder on $\mathcal E_{z,R}$. It gives $\lambda_m(\mathcal E_{z,R})^{1-1/p}
(\sum_w\int F_w^p\,d\lambda_m)^{1/p}$, bounded by the same expression. The radius exponents are $m(1-1/p)+\beta-m(1-1/p)=\beta$. The densities in this calculation represent exact observations, so the measurable routing functions are evaluated at the exact labels. ◻

</details>

The exact density can be replaced in this step by Proposition <a href="#h:dv:measurable-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:measurable-test">23</a> and the test form of Lemma <a href="#h:alt:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:alt:selection">18</a>. For spherical restrictions, Propositions <a href="#h:dv:joint-l1" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:joint-l1">26</a> and <a href="#h:dv:pointwise-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:pointwise-density">27</a> give the same norm. Likewise, either enclosing-ball calculation can replace the exact ellipsoid volume. These choices change only the absolute constant, not the coefficient or radius powers.

<a id="stmt-h-ev-coefficient-success"></a>
<div id="h:ev:coefficient-success" class="proposition">

<a id="h:ev:common-success"></a>
**Proposition 34** (A common finite success bound). *Assume $d\ge16$, set $n=d-1$, $p=\lfloor d/8\rfloor$, and let $n/2\le\beta\le d/2$. Let $M,T\ge0$ be integers, $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, and consider a learner in Definition <a href="#h:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:model">1</a>. Under the uniform-sphere signal prior, write $P_{\mathrm{succ}}$ for its success probability with either angular or chordal error at most $\epsilon$, and put $b=\lceil T/p\rceil$. Then $$\label{h:ev:common-success}
 P_{\mathrm{succ}}\le
 \min\left\{1,\,
 4^n\epsilon^{n-\beta}
 \left[C_{\mathrm{step}}^d
       \bigl((p+2)2^M\bigr)^{1/p}\right]^b\right\}.$$ The same assertion holds with the initial factor $4^n$ replaced by the polar-cap constant $C_0^d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-coefficient-success"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-coefficient-success</summary>

*Proof.* Use the seedwise conditional experiment and Borel replacement from Section <a href="#h:stream:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:reduction">5.2</a>. Conditional on almost every shared seed, the initial state remains independent of the signal and rows. Lemma <a href="#h:stream:categories" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:categories">14</a> supplies, from every specified boundary state, including an unreachable one, Borel routes to fresh continuations. In a block of $m$ rows their number is at most $(m+2)2^M$: continuing states and terminal states tagged with one of the $m+1$ local stopping indices. The lemma applies to either Borel terminal score.

<a id="h:ev:coefficient-recursion"></a>
The terminal bound <a href="#h:dv:terminal-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:terminal-coefficient">[h:dv:terminal-coefficient]</a> starts the all-radius coefficient at $H_0=4^n\epsilon^{n-\beta}$. With $N=(p+2)2^M$, Proposition <a href="#h:ev:coefficient-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:coefficient-step">33</a> gives one recurrence for all actual block lengths $1\le m\le p$: $$\label{h:ev:coefficient-recursion}
 H_j=H_0\bigl(C_{\mathrm{step}}^dN^{1/p}\bigr)^j.$$ Each multiplier is at least one, so terminal options, whose coefficient is $H_0$, satisfy every later bound. The ball $B(0,1)$ contains the whole sphere; hence the initial coefficient bounds total success. Average first over the independent conditional initialization and then over the original seed experiment. As in Section <a href="#h:stream:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:reduction">5.2</a>, this averages original conditional probabilities and requires no jointly measurable choice of the seedwise replacements. The trivial probability bound gives the minimum with one. For $T=0$ this is the terminal estimate. Starting instead from <a href="#h:ev:polar-terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:polar-terminal">[h:ev:polar-terminal]</a> proves the polar version.

One may instead pad the last block of actual length $m<p$ with independent Gaussian rows and extend each route by ignoring them. Its matrix–label marginal on the first $m$ rows is unchanged. Thus full padded blocks give exactly the same estimate and count. ◻

</details>

The two endpoint exponents recover the following bounds.

<a id="stmt-h-ev-success"></a>
<div id="h:ev:success" class="corollary">

<a id="h:ev:half-sphere-success"></a>
**Corollary 35** (Finite success bounds for the two coefficients). *Assume $d\ge16$ and $p=\lfloor d/8\rfloor$. Let $M,T\ge0$ be integers, let $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, and consider a learner in Definition <a href="#h:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:model">1</a> with these parameters. Draw $S\sim\sigma$ independently of its Gaussian rows $x_1,\ldots,x_T$ and its data-independent randomness, and set the exact labels $y_t=\langle x_t,S\rangle$. Write $\Pr_\sigma$ for the resulting joint law and put $b=\lceil T/p\rceil$. With an absolute $C_{\mathrm{step}}\ge1$, the uniform-prior angular success probability satisfies $$\label{h:ev:half-sphere-success}
 \Pr_\sigma\{\operatorname{angle}(\widehat S,S)\le\epsilon\}
 \le
 \min\!\left\{1,\,
 4^d\epsilon^{(d-1)/2}
 \left[C_{\mathrm{step}}^d
       \bigl((p+2)2^M\bigr)^{1/p}\right]^b
 \right\}.$$ The uniform-prior chordal success probability satisfies <a id="h:ev:half-ambient-success"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-ambient-success}
 \Pr_\sigma\{\|\widehat S-S\|\le\epsilon\}
 \le
 \min\!\left\{1,\,
 C_0^d\epsilon^{d/2-1}
 \left[C_{\mathrm{step}}^d
       \bigl((2p+4)2^M\bigr)^{1/p}\right]^b
 \right\}.$$ The second event contains the angular success event.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-ev-success"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-ev-success</summary>

*Proof.* In Proposition <a href="#h:ev:coefficient-success" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:coefficient-success">34</a>, set $\beta=n/2$ and use $4^n\le4^d$ for the first formula. For the second, set $\beta=d/2$, use the polar terminal coefficient, and enlarge the category bound to $N=(2p+4)2^M$. The same recurrence then reads <a id="h:ev:half-ambient-recursion"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-ambient-recursion}
 H_j=C_0^d\epsilon^{d/2-1}
       \bigl(C_{\mathrm{step}}^dN^{1/p}\bigr)^j.$$ The final ball $B(0,1)$ gives the second formula. Angular success implies chordal success. ◻

</details>

<a id="h:dv:applications"></a>
### Normalizations and quantitative consequences

The common recurrence separates choices that need not be coupled: the local exponent, the exact-label passage, the common support, and the use of actual or padded blocks. This section records their substitutions and extracts the sample bounds once. Throughout, $d\ge16$, $n=d-1$, $p=\lfloor d/8\rfloor$, and $b=\lceil T/p\rceil$. The nearby integer $p$ need not equal the moment order $q$ used in the main proof. All success probabilities average the uniform signal, Gaussian rows, and learner randomness. A success guarantee for every signal implies these premises by averaging.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### The density interfaces in the same recurrence

For $n/2\le\beta\le d/2$, a restricted continuation measure $\mu_w=\mathbf1_{B(z,R)}h_w\sigma_d$ with ambient coefficient $H$ has an exact joint density satisfying <a id="h:dv:application-moment"></a>$$\label{h:dv:application-moment}
 \int F_w^p\,d\lambda_m
 \le C_{\mathrm D}^{d(p-1)}
       H^pR^{\beta p-m(p-1)}
 \qquad(1\le m\le p).$$ This is Lemma <a href="#h:ev:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:density">29</a>; the joint-$L^1$ or pointwise construction gives the same bound. Either $\lambda_m(\mathcal E_{z,R})\le C_{\mathrm E}^dR^m$ or $\lambda_m(\mathcal S_{z,R})\le C_{\mathrm V}^dR^m$ restores $R^\beta$ in Lemma <a href="#h:alt:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:alt:selection">18</a>. Thus no normalization to a posterior and no repeated backward induction is needed.

<a id="h:dv:half-ambient-application"></a>
###### Direct measurable tests.

At $\beta=d/2,m=p$, the inverse-distance bound for an affine $j$-plane, $0\le j\le p-2$, has the explicit form $$\int\operatorname{dist}(s,L)^{-p}\,d\mu(s)
 \le\left(1+3^j2^{d/2}\frac{p}{d/2-j-p}\right)
       H R^{d/2-p},
 \qquad d/2-j-p\ge d/4 .$$ Proposition <a href="#h:dv:measurable-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:measurable-test">23</a> therefore supplies the test scale $C^dHR^{d/2-p(1-1/p)}$, without using a strong density conclusion. The full-block volume <a href="#h:ev:full-ellipsoid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:full-ellipsoid">[h:ev:full-ellipsoid]</a> and the test form of Lemma <a href="#h:alt:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:alt:selection">18</a> give the same coefficient step. The hemisphere terminal coefficient and count $(p+2)2^M$ give <a href="#h:ev:common-success" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:common-success">[h:ev:common-success]</a> at this exponent, and hence the base-two bounds below for either success event.

<a id="h:dv:half-sphere-application"></a>
###### Joint $L^1$ convergence.

<a id="h:dv:l1-success"></a>
At $\beta=n/2$, Proposition <a href="#h:dv:joint-l1" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:joint-l1">26</a> applies for every actual row count $1\le m\le p$, since $\mu_w\ll\sigma_d$ and $m<d$. With the polar terminal coefficient, either support estimate and the common recurrence give $$\label{h:dv:l1-success}
 \Pr_\sigma\{\operatorname{angle}(\widehat S,S)\le\epsilon\}
 \le C_0^d\epsilon^{n/2}
 \left[C_{\mathrm{L1}}^d\bigl((p+2)2^M\bigr)^{1/p}\right]^b$$ for an absolute $C_{\mathrm{L1}}$. Neither the quarter-net proof nor a particular polar denominator interval is required for this density passage.

<a id="h:dv:factored-application"></a>
###### Factored accuracy.

Writing $H=D\epsilon^{n-\beta}$ moves the fixed accuracy factor outside <a href="#h:ev:coefficient-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:coefficient-recursion">[h:ev:coefficient-recursion]</a>; the recurrence simply multiplies $D$ by $C^dN^{1/p}$. For example, at $\beta=n/2,m=p$, the specified pointwise density of Proposition <a href="#h:dv:pointwise-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:dv:pointwise-density">27</a> satisfies <a id="h:dv:factored-moment"></a>$$\label{h:dv:factored-moment}
 \int(F_w^\sharp)^p\,d\lambda_p
 \le
 \bigl(D\epsilon^{n/2}R^{n/2}\bigr)^p
 \bigl(C_3^dR^{-p}\bigr)^{p-1}.$$ This follows by substituting $H=D\epsilon^{n/2}$ in <a href="#h:dv:pointwise-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:pointwise-moment">[h:dv:pointwise-moment]</a>. The corresponding inverse-distance bound is $C_3^dD\epsilon^{n/2}R^{n/2-p}$, with margin at least $d/4$. Either net volume or the ellipsoid volume cancels the negative radius power. The valid looser category count $\mathcal N=2(p+1)2^M\ge(p+2)2^M$ consequently gives $$\Pr_\sigma\{\operatorname{angle}(\widehat S,S)\le\epsilon\}
 \le C_0^d\bigl(C^d\mathcal N^{1/p}\bigr)^b\epsilon^{n/2}.$$ This enlargement has no connection to the pointwise fiber quantifier; all category counts concern only local stopping offsets, not $T$.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### One logarithmic extraction

<a id="h:dv:log-extraction"></a>
Suppose an upper bound obtained above has the form $$P_{\mathrm{succ}}\le K\epsilon^\kappa e^{d b\Lambda},
 \qquad K>0,\quad\kappa,\Lambda>0 .$$ If $P_{\mathrm{succ}}\ge2/3$, taking natural logarithms and using $b\le T/p+1$ gives $$\label{h:dv:log-extraction}
 b\ge
 \frac{\kappa\log(1/\epsilon)-\log K-\log(3/2)}{d\Lambda},
 \qquad
 \frac Tp\ge
 \frac{\kappa\log(1/\epsilon)-\log K-\log(3/2)}{d\Lambda}-1.$$ All the exponents above satisfy $\kappa=n-\beta\ge d/3$. The negative constant in this inequality is the rounded-block loss. The full-data reflection estimate handles the remaining bounded logarithms; the following elementary consequences retain several useful numerical endpoints.

<a id="h:ev:reflection-sixteenth"></a>
<a id="h:ev:reflection-seven-twelfths"></a>
For a learner with a deterministic sample limit $T$, use the fixed-seed Borel experiment of Section <a href="#h:stream:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:stream:reduction">5.2</a>. Pre-generate all $T$ Gaussian rows and grant it the full data; its stopped output law is then a kernel of those rows and labels. Apply Lemma <a href="#h:ev:reflection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:ev:reflection">17</a> conditionally and average the original conditional probabilities and expectations, as in the main endpoint argument. If its angular success probability is at least $2/3$ and $\epsilon\le1/10$, then $$\mathbb E\langle\widehat S,S\rangle
 \ge \frac23\cos(1/10)-\frac13>\frac14.$$ Together with <a href="#h:ev:reflection-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:reflection-expectation">[h:ev:reflection-expectation]</a> this proves $$\label{h:ev:reflection-sixteenth}
 T>d/16.$$ For chordal success, $T\le d/8$ and $b_0=3/4$ in <a href="#h:ev:antipodal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:antipodal">[h:ev:antipodal]</a> give $$\label{h:ev:reflection-seven-twelfths}
 \Pr\{\|\widehat S-S\|\le\epsilon\}\le\frac7{12}
 \qquad(\epsilon\le1/10).$$ Equivalently, Markov’s inequality bounds $\Pr\{\|u\|^2\ge3/4\}$ by $1/6$; on its complement the reflected points have distance greater than one, and at most half of that mass can succeed. The choices $b_0=1/2$ and $T\le d/8$ give $5/8$. Formula <a href="#h:ev:direct-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:direct-residual">[h:ev:direct-residual]</a> also shows that $T\le d/5$ is incompatible with success $2/3$ when $\epsilon\le1/10$.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### Explicit constants and thresholds

All statements here assume $0<\epsilon\le1/10$ and success at least $2/3$, with angular success sufficient whenever the displayed upper bound uses chordal success. Constants may be enlarged to cover any of the preceding density or support calculations.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
###### Half the spherical dimension.

For $M\le d^2$, the count satisfies $$\bigl((p+2)2^M\bigr)^{1/p}\le2^{32d},$$ since $p+2\le2^p$ and $M/p+1\le16d+1\le32d$. Choose $D\ge\max\{4,e\}$ so that the per-block factor in <a href="#h:ev:half-sphere-success" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:half-sphere-success">[h:ev:half-sphere-success]</a> is at most $D^d$. Then <a id="h:ev:half-sphere-absolute"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-sphere-absolute}
 \Pr_\sigma\{\operatorname{angle}(\widehat S,S)\le\epsilon\}
 \le D^{d(b+1)}\epsilon^{n/2}.$$ Set $L=\log(1/\epsilon)$ and $c_0=(3\log D)^{-1}$. Substitution of $K=D^d,\Lambda=\log D,\kappa=n/2$ into <a href="#h:dv:log-extraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:log-extraction">[h:dv:log-extraction]</a> gives $b\ge c_0L-2$ and $T\ge p(c_0L-3)$. Hence <a id="h:ev:half-sphere-threshold"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-sphere-threshold}
 L\ge6/c_0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 T\ge\frac d{16}\frac{c_0}{2}L.$$ For $L<6/c_0$, <a href="#h:ev:reflection-sixteenth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:reflection-sixteenth">[h:ev:reflection-sixteenth]</a> gives $T>(c_0/96)dL$.

Equivalently, absorb the initial and block factors into $e^{C_*d}$, $C_*\ge\max\{1,\log4\}$. The substitution $K=e^{C_*d},\Lambda=C_*,\kappa=n/2$ gives <a id="h:ev:exponential-threshold"></a>$$\label{h:ev:exponential-threshold}
 \frac Tp\ge\frac{L}{3C_*}-3,\qquad
 L\ge18C_*\quad\Longrightarrow\quad T\ge\frac{dL}{96C_*}.$$ This also applies to <a href="#h:dv:l1-success" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:l1-success">[h:dv:l1-success]</a> when $M=o(d^2)$: its logarithmic category cost is $o(d)$, so an absolute $C_*$ works after a dimension threshold depending on that memory sequence. The direct residual estimate rules out $T\le d/5$ for the bounded complementary range.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
###### Half the ambient dimension.

For $M=o(d^2)$, $\log((2p+4)2^M)/p=o(d)$. Thus an absolute $A\ge e$, after an eventual dimension threshold depending on the memory sequence, gives <a id="h:ev:half-ambient-absolute"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-ambient-absolute}
 \Pr_\sigma\{\|\widehat S-S\|\le\epsilon\}
 \le A^{d(b+1)}\epsilon^{d/2-1}.$$ With $a_0=(3\log A)^{-1}$, substitute $K=A^d,\Lambda=\log A,\kappa=d/2-1$ in <a href="#h:dv:log-extraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:log-extraction">[h:dv:log-extraction]</a> to obtain <a id="h:ev:half-ambient-log"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-ambient-log}
 b\ge a_0L-2,\qquad T\ge p(a_0L-3),$$ and therefore <a id="h:ev:half-ambient-threshold"></a>$$\label{h:ev:half-ambient-threshold}
 L\ge6/a_0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 T\ge\frac d{16}\frac{a_0}{2}L.$$ For $L<6/a_0$, <a href="#h:ev:reflection-seven-twelfths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:ev:reflection-seven-twelfths">[h:ev:reflection-seven-twelfths]</a> forces $T>d/8>(a_0/48)dL$.

For the base-two form, use instead the hemisphere coefficient $4^{d-1}\epsilon^{d/2-1}$ and the count $(p+2)2^M$. When $M\le d^2$, an absolute $C_1\ge1$ bounds the block factor by $2^{C_1d}$. With $L_2=\log_2(1/\epsilon)$, the same logarithmic extraction, using $2(d-1)-\log_2(2/3)\le3d$, gives <a id="h:ev:binary-endgame"></a>$$\label{h:ev:binary-endgame}
 \Pr_\sigma\{\|\widehat S-S\|\le\epsilon\}
 \le2^{C_1db}4^{d-1}\epsilon^{d/2-1},
 \qquad
 \frac Tp\ge\frac{L_2/3-3}{C_1}-1.$$ In particular, <a id="h:ev:binary-threshold"></a>$$\label{h:ev:binary-threshold}
 L_2\ge6(3+C_1)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 T\ge\frac{dL_2}{96C_1}.$$ Here $L_2/3-3-C_1\ge L_2/6$ and $p\ge d/16$. In the complementary range, the antipodal choice $b_0=1/2$ gives $5/8<2/3$ when $T\le d/8$. These formulas also apply to the direct-test route above.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
###### The accuracy-factored form.

For $M=o(d^2)$, the count in the factored recurrence obeys $\mathcal N^{1/p}\le e^d$ eventually. For absolute constants $C_4,C_5$ it follows that <a id="h:dv:factored-endpoint"></a>$$\label{h:dv:factored-endpoint}
 \Pr_\sigma\{\operatorname{angle}(\widehat S,S)\le\epsilon\}
 \le e^{C_4d(b+1)}\epsilon^{(d-1)/2},
 \qquad
 \log(1/\epsilon)\le C_5(T/p+2).$$ The second inequality is <a href="#h:dv:log-extraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:dv:log-extraction">[h:dv:log-extraction]</a>, with $n/2\ge d/3$ and the fixed $\log(3/2)$ absorbed into $C_5$. Reflection gives $T>d/8$, and $p\ge d/16$ then gives $T/p+2<32T/d$. Consequently $T\ge d\log(1/\epsilon)/(32C_5)$ over the entire accuracy range, without a relation between precision and memory.

For each fixed $A_0>0$, replacing the memory condition by $M\le A_0d^2$ changes only the constants and bounded-logarithm thresholds, which may depend on $A_0$. These arguments do not give a uniform positive constant for unbounded $A_0=A_0(d)$.

<a id="h:end:tail-section"></a>
### A sharper full-data residual bound

The reflection estimate suffices for the main theorem. The following bound gives a sharper success probability by identifying the full conditional residual sphere. It allows arbitrary data-based Euclidean outputs, including randomized ones.

<a id="stmt-h-end-linear"></a>
<div id="h:end:linear" class="lemma">

<a id="h:end:full-data-bound"></a>
**Lemma 36** (A memory-independent constant-accuracy bound). *For all sufficiently large $d$, let $0\le T\le d/4$ be an integer. Let $S$ be uniform on $S^{d-1}$, let $X$ be an independent $T$-by-$d$ standard Gaussian matrix, and put $Y=XS$. For every $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, any possibly randomized estimator $W\in\mathbb R^d$ based on the entire pair $(X,Y)$ satisfies $$\label{h:end:full-data-bound}
 \mathbb P\{\|W-S\|\le\epsilon\}
 \le \frac13+\left(\frac25\right)^{3d/4-1}
 <\frac23.$$ Consequently, a learner with uniform-sphere angular success at least $2/3$ at accuracy $\epsilon\le1/10$ must have $T>d/4$, even with unlimited memory and access to all pre-generated samples.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-h-end-linear"><summary>Proof of stmt-h-end-linear</summary>

*Proof.* Let $U$ be the row span of $X$, write $r=\dim U\le T$, and put $k=d-r$. The labels determine $u=P_US$: the restriction of $X$ to $U=(\ker X)^\perp$ is injective. More explicitly, when $T>0$, diagonalizing $XX^{\mathsf T}$ shows that the consistent label $Y$ determines $$u=\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}
 X^{\mathsf T}(XX^{\mathsf T}+\eta I_T)^{-1}Y.$$ This also shows that $u$ is a measurable function of the data. The analogous limit $$P_U=\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}X^{\mathsf T}
 (XX^{\mathsf T}+\eta I_T)^{-1}X$$ shows that the projection is measurable in $X$. For the empty horizon $T=0$, $X$ has no rows and $U=\{0\}$, $u=0$. The same values hold whenever the row span is zero, including an all-zero matrix. In all these cases the residual radius $\rho=\sqrt{1-\|u\|^2}$ equals one.

Standard Borel disintegration supplies a conditional probability kernel for the actual joint law and the Borel data map $(X,S)\mapsto(X,XS)$ (Gagné and Panangaden 2023, Theorem 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[GagnePanangaden2023](#ref-GagnePanangaden2023)]. To identify it almost everywhere, condition first on any fixed matrix $X$ with $r<d$, and generate $S$ as $G/\|G\|$ from an independent standard Gaussian vector. Its orthogonal components $G_U$ and $G_{U^\perp}$ are independent. In polar coordinates on $U^\perp$, the Gaussian volume element factors as a constant times $e^{-\varrho^2/2}\varrho^{k-1}\,d\varrho\,d\omega$ for $\varrho>0$, where $d\omega$ is spherical area. Hence $$V=\frac{G_{U^\perp}}{\|G_{U^\perp}\|}$$ is uniform on the unit sphere of $U^\perp$ and is independent of $(G_U,\|G_{U^\perp}\|)$. Here $\|G_{U^\perp}\|>0$ almost surely because $k=d-r\ge d-T>0$. The decomposition $$S=u+\rho V,\qquad
 u=\frac{G_U}{\sqrt{\|G_U\|^2+\|G_{U^\perp}\|^2}},
 \qquad
 \rho=\frac{\|G_{U^\perp}\|}
 {\sqrt{\|G_U\|^2+\|G_{U^\perp}\|^2}}$$ shows that $V$ is independent of $(u,\rho)$ conditional on $X$. Since $u$ and $\rho$ are determined by $(X,Y)$, the conditional law of the residual signal given the data is uniform on the sphere of radius $\rho$ in $U^\perp$, for almost every data pair. When $U=\{0\}$ this is the whole unit sphere and $\rho=1$. This argument uses the Gaussian direction directly, including that zero-rank case.

<a id="h:end:radius"></a>
Independence of $X$ and $S$, together with $\mathbb E SS^{\mathsf T}=I_d/d$, gives $$\mathbb E\|u\|^2
 =\mathbb E\frac{\dim U}{d}
 \le\frac Td\le\frac14.$$ Markov’s inequality therefore yields $$\label{h:end:radius}
 \mathbb P\{\rho<1/2\}
 =\mathbb P\{\|u\|^2>3/4\}\le\frac13.$$ On data with $\rho\ge1/2$, fix any proposed estimate $w\in\mathbb R^d$. If $\|w-S\|\le\epsilon$, orthogonal projection onto $U^\perp$ and division by $\rho$ imply $$\left\|V-\frac{P_{U^\perp}w}{\rho}\right\|
 \le\frac{\epsilon}{\rho}\le\frac15.$$ If this ball meets the unit sphere of $U^\perp$, choose a point of the intersection. The entire intersection lies in the ball of radius $2/5$ about that sphere point. Lemma <a href="#h:local:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="h:local:caps">5</a>, applied in ambient dimension $k\ge d-T\ge3d/4$, bounds its conditional spherical probability by $$\left(\frac25\right)^{k-1}
 \le\left(\frac25\right)^{3d/4-1}.$$ An empty intersection has probability zero. This bound holds for each $w$, so it also holds after averaging any output probability kernel based on the data. Combining it with <a href="#h:end:radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:end:radius">[h:end:radius]</a> proves <a href="#h:end:full-data-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="h:end:full-data-bound">[h:end:full-data-bound]</a> for large $d$. Finally, for unit vectors angular error at most $\epsilon$ implies Euclidean error at most $\epsilon$, since $2\sin(\theta/2)\le\theta$. Granting all pre-generated rows lets a full-data estimator simulate any earlier stopping rule, which proves the stated consequence. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The backward block estimate separates the exact projection density from its common support volume. Hölder combines their radius powers to recover $r^{a}$, while the routing constraint charges $N^{1/q}$ for the number of destinations. Absolute constants $C$ may change between bounds.",
      "figure_id": "h:fig:block-scaling",
      "pdf_page": 13,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "h:fig:block-scaling",
          "exact_quote": "The backward block estimate separates the exact projection\ndensity from its common support volume. H\\\"older combines their radius\npowers to recover \\(r^a\\), while the routing constraint charges\n\\(N^{1/q}\\) for the number of destinations. Ab",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n box/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,text width=5.8cm,\n             inner sep=4pt,font=\\small},\n arrow/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},thick},node distance=5mm and 4mm]\n\\node[box] (local) {Future success at scale \\(R\\)\\\\\n \\(\\displaystyle\\int_{B(z,r)}h_j\\,d\\sigma\\le(Rr)^a\\)\\\\\n for every sphere center \\(z\\) and \\(r>0\\)};\n\\node[box,below=of local] (measure) {Restrict successful mass to \\(B(z,r)\\)\\\\\n \\(\\mu_j=\\mathbf1_{B(z,r)}h_j\\sigma\\)};\n\\node[box,below=of measure,xshift=-3.1cm] (moment) {Exact density from local mass\\\\\n \\(\\|f_j\\|_q\\le e^{Cd}R^a r^{a-m(1-1/q)}\\)};\n\\node[box,below=of measure,xshift=3.1cm] (support) {Exact ellipsoid support\\\\\n \\(\\lambda_m(\\mathcal E_{z,r})\\le C_{\\mathrm E}^d r^m\\)};\n\\node[box,below=22mm of measure] (holder) {H\\\"older restores \\(r^a\\) and costs \\(N^{1/q}\\)\\\\\n \\(\\displaystyle\\int_{B(z,r)}h\\,d\\sigma\\le e^{Cd}N^{1/q}(Rr)^a\\)};\n\\node[box,below=of holder] (output) {The preceding state has local scale\\\\\n \\(R'=R\\exp(Cd/a)N^{1/(aq)}\\)};\n\\draw[arrow] (local)--(measure);\n\\draw[arrow] (measure)--(moment);\n\\draw[arrow] (measure)--(support);\n\\draw[arrow] (moment)--(holder);\n\\draw[arrow] (support)--(holder);\n\\draw[arrow] (holder)--(output);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A vertical flowchart illustrating the backward block estimate update:\n- Top rectangular box: contains the text \"Future success at scale $R$\", followed below by the displayed formula $\\int_{B(z,r)} h_j \\, d\\sigma \\le (Rr)^a$, followed below by \"for every sphere center $z$ and $r > 0$\".\n- A vertical downward arrow points to the second rectangular box: contains the text \"Restrict successful mass to $B(z, r)$\" and the equation $\\mu_j = \\mathbf{1}_{B(z,r)} h_j \\sigma$.\n- From the bottom of the second box, two downward arrows branch out to two side-by-side rectangular boxes:\n  - Left box: contains the text \"Exact density from local mass\" and the inequality $\\|f_j\\|_q \\le e^{Cd} R^a r^{a-m(1-1/q)}$.\n  - Right box: contains the text \"Exact ellipsoid support\" and the inequality $\\lambda_m(\\mathcal{E}_{z,r}) \\le C_E^d r^m$.\n- Downward arrows from the bottom of both the left and right boxes converge into a fourth rectangular box: contains the text \"Hölder restores $r^a$ and costs $N^{1/q}$\" and the displayed formula $\\int_{B(z,r)} h \\, d\\sigma \\le e^{Cd} N^{1/q} (Rr)^a$.\n- A vertical downward arrow leads from the fourth box to the bottom rectangular box: contains the text \"The preceding state has local scale\" and the formula $R' = R \\exp(Cd/a) N^{1/(aq)}$."
    }
  ],
